On the other hand, the corresponding straightenedness for bundle inner products is a consequence of requiring that the connection preserves the inner product. Therefore we do not need a more specific definition.

Now we consider the case with group actions.

Definition 2.31
.

Let
(U,g^{TU})
be a Riemannian
G
-manifold. Let
E\to U
be a
G
-equivariant vector bundle equipped with a
G
-invariant connection
\nabla^{E}
, a
G
-invariant inner product
h^{E}
, and an NC structure
{\bm{I}}^{E}
.

(1)

We say that
\nabla^{E}
is
straightened
with respect to
g^{TU}
if for each
U
-essential subgroup
H\subseteq G
,
\nabla^{E}
is straightened along
NU_{H}
.

(2)

We say that
{\bm{I}}^{E}
is
straightened
(with respect to
g^{TU}
and
\nabla^{E}
) if for each
U
-essential subgroup
H\subseteq G
, the bundle map
{\rm par}^{NS}
sends the pullback NC structure on
\pi_{NU_{H}}^{*}(E|_{U_{H}})
to the NC structure on
E|_{|N^{\epsilon}U_{H}|}
.

(3)

We say that the triple
(\nabla^{E},h^{E},{\bm{I}}^{E})
is
normally Hermitian
if for each
H\subseteq_{U}G
, the restriction
h^{E}|_{\check{E}_{H}}
is Hermitian with respect to
I^{\check{E}_{H}}
, and the induced connection
\nabla^{\check{E}_{H}}
is a Hermitian connection.

Notice that the bundle isomorphism
{\rm par}^{NS}
is defined by parallel transport. Then if for all
H\subseteq_{U}G
,
\nabla^{\check{E}_{H}}
is complex linear with respect to
I^{\check{E}_{H}}
, then
{\bm{I}}^{E}
is straightened. In particular, one has the following situation.

Lemma 2.32
.

If
E\to U
is a
G
-equivariant complex vector space with complex structure
I^{E}
. Let
{\bm{I}}^{E}
be the induced NC structure. Let
h^{E}
be a Hermitian inner product and
\nabla^{E}
is a Hermitian connection. Then
{\bm{I}}^{E}
is straightened with respect to
g^{TU}
and
\nabla^{E}
and the triple
(\nabla^{E},h^{E},{\bm{I}}^{E})
is normally Hermitian.

The extension of these notions to orbifold vector bundles is straightforward.

Definition 2.33
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},g^{T{\mathcal{U}}})
be a Riemannian orbifold. Let
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be an orbifold vector bundle equipped with a connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
, an inner product
h^{\mathcal{E}}
, and an NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{{\mathcal{E}}}
.

(1)

We say that
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
is
straightened
with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
if its pullback to each chart
\hat{C}=(G,U,E,\hat{\psi})
is a straightened connection
\nabla^{E}
with respect to the chartwise metric
g^{TU}
.

(2)

We say that
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
is
straightened
with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
if its pullback to each chart is straightened with respect to the chartwise pullbacks of the bundle connection.

(3)

We say that the triple
(\nabla^{\mathcal{E}},h^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}})
is
normally Hermitian
if its pullback to each bundle chart
\hat{C}=(G,U,E,\hat{\psi})
is normally Hermitian.

Then one has the existence result for straightened structures. Its proof is simpler than the case of straightened Riemannian metrics.

Proposition 2.34
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},g^{T{\mathcal{U}}})
be a straightened Riemannian orbifold. Let
{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a closed subset. Let
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be a vector bundle equipped with an NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
. Let
\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
be a connection on
{\mathcal{E}}
,
h_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
be an inner product on
{\mathcal{E}}
, and
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
be an NC structure on
{\mathcal{E}}
, satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is close to
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
.

(2)

\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is straightened near
{\mathcal{Y}}
with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
.

(3)

h_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is preserved by
\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
near
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

(4)

{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is straightened with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and
\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
near
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

(5)

(\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}},h_{0}^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}})
is normally Hermitian near
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Then there exist a connection
\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
, an inner product
h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
, and an NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
is close to
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
.

(2)

\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
is straightened with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
.

(3)

h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
is preserved by
\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
.

(4)

{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
is straightened with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and
\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
.

(5)

(\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}})
is normally Hermitian.

(6)

(\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}})
coincides with
(\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}},h_{0}^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}})
near
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

In addition, if
{\mathcal{E}}
is a complex vector bundle,
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
is the NC structure induced from the complex structure,
h_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is a Hermitian inner product,
\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is a Hermitian connection, and
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
, then we can require that
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
,
h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}=h_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
, and
\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
is Hermitian.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.2
.
∎

Choosing a system of the above “straightened” structures are the prerequisite for the FOP perturbation method. We introduce the following concept.

Definition 2.35
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}})
be an NC orbifold and
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}})
be an NC vector bundle over
{\mathcal{U}}
. A
straightening
of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
is a pair
({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
where

(1)

{\mathcal{U}}^{\#}=({\mathcal{U}},g_{1}^{T{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{T{\mathcal{U}}})
consists of a straightened Riemannian metric
g_{1}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{U}}
and an NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
which is straightened with respect to
g_{1}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and which is concordant to
{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
, and

(2)

{\mathcal{E}}^{\#}=({\mathcal{E}},\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}})
consists of a connection
\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
on
{\mathcal{E}}
which is straightened with respect to
g_{1}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
,
h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
is an inner product on
{\mathcal{E}}
which is preserved by
\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
, and
{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
is an NC structure on
{\mathcal{E}}
which is straightened with respect to
g_{1}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and
\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}
and which is concordant to
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
, such that
(\nabla_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},h_{1}^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}_{1}^{\mathcal{E}})
is normally Hermitian.

Remark 2.36
.

Later we will see that the bundle inner product will not enter the definition of normally complex
perturbation.

The following corollary is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.29
and Proposition
2.34
.

Corollary 2.37
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
be an NC pair and
{\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a closed subset. Suppose a straightening of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
is given over a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{Y}}
. Then there exists a straightening of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
which agrees with the given one near
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Another direct corollary to Proposition
2.29
and Proposition
2.34
is that two straightenings can be connected in a one-parameter family.

Corollary 2.38
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}})
be an NC orbifold without boundary. Let
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}})
be an NC vector bundle over
{\mathcal{U}}
. Let

\Big({\mathcal{U}}_{i}^{\#}=({\mathcal{U}},g_{i}^{T{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}),\ {\mathcal{E}}_{i}^{\#}=({\mathcal{E}},\nabla_{i}^{\mathcal{E}},h_{i}^{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}})\Big),\ i=0,1

be two straightenings of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}={\mathcal{U}}\times[0,1]
equipped with the product NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{T\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}\times[0,1]
equipped with the product NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}
. Then there exists a straightening
(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\#},\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
of
(\tilde{\mathcal{U}},\tilde{\mathcal{E}})
whose restriction to
{\mathcal{U}}\times[0,\epsilon)
resp.
{\mathcal{U}}\times(1-\epsilon,1]
coincides with the product of
({\mathcal{U}}_{0}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}_{0}^{\#})
resp.
({\mathcal{U}}_{1}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\#})
with the interval
[0,\epsilon)
resp.
(1-\epsilon,1]
.

Proof.

We first choose a concordance of the NC structures. As
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
resp.
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}}
is concordant to
{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
resp.
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
, there exists an NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}_{01}^{T\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
on
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
resp.
{\mathcal{I}}_{01}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}
on
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
which is concordant to
{\mathcal{I}}^{T\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}
resp.
{\mathcal{I}}^{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}}
and which agrees with the product of
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
resp.
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}^{\mathcal{E}}
near the corresponding boundaries. For
\epsilon
sufficiently small, denote
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{U}}\times([0,\epsilon]\cup[1-\epsilon,1])
. Then the desired straightening can be constructed using Proposition
2.29
and Proposition
2.34
.
∎

2.4.3.
Straightenings and stabilizations

Recall that for an NC pair
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
and an NC vector bundle
{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
, the stabilization is

({\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}).

Upon choosing a connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{F}}
on
{\mathcal{F}}
, the stabilization becomes an NC pair. We need to consider straightenings on the stabilization. First, similar to the manifold case, if
{\mathcal{U}}
is equipped with a metric
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and if
h^{\mathcal{F}}
is an inner product on
{\mathcal{F}}
preserved by
\nabla^{\mathcal{F}}
, then on the total space
{\mathcal{F}}
there is a canonically induced
bundle metric

g^{T{\mathcal{F}}}
. There are similar pullback connections.

Proposition 2.39
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
be a straightened NC pair with domain metric
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
, the domain NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
, the bundle connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
, the bundle inner product
h^{\mathcal{E}}
, and the bundle NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
.

Let
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be a vector bundle equipped with an inner product
h^{\mathcal{F}}
, an
h^{\mathcal{F}}
-preserving connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{F}}
, and an NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{F}}
such that the triple
(\nabla^{\mathcal{F}},h^{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{F}})
is normally Hermitian (Definition
2.33
). Suppose
\nabla^{\mathcal{F}}
is straightened with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
. Consider the pair

({\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}})

Then the domain metric
g^{T{\mathcal{F}}}
as the bundle metric on
{\mathcal{F}}
, the domain NC structure as the bundle NC structure on
{\mathcal{F}}
, the bundle connection
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
, the bundle inner product
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}h^{\mathcal{E}}
, the bundle NC structure
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
give a straightening of
({\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}})
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.3
.
∎

2.4.4.
Suborbifolds with ordinary normal bundle

Now we discuss extending straightenings on an suborbifold with ordinary normal bundle to the ambient orbifold.

We first reorganize the notations. Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be an orbifold and
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a suborbifold with ordinary normal bundle. Recall that for each chart
C=(G,U,\psi)
,
X:=\psi^{-1}({\mathcal{X}})\subseteq U
is a
G
-invariant closed submanifold. Let
\pi^{N{\mathcal{X}}}:N{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
be the normal bundle and
N^{\epsilon}{\mathcal{X}}
is the disk bundle of radius
\epsilon:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. If
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
is a Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{U}}
, then there is a normal exponential map

\exp^{N{\mathcal{X}}}:N^{\epsilon}{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{U}}

which is an open embedding of orbifolds. Notice that the total space
N{\mathcal{X}}
carries a bundle metric. When
{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{X}}}
is an NC structure on
{\mathcal{X}}
induced from an NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
, there is a bundle NC structure on
N{\mathcal{X}}
.

In addition, if
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is a vector bundle and
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
is a connection on
{\mathcal{E}}
. Abbreviate
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}}:={\mathcal{E}}|_{\mathcal{X}}
. Then along normal geodesics, there is a bundle map covering the normal exponential map

{\rm par}^{N{\mathcal{X}}}:\Big(\pi_{N{\mathcal{X}}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}}\Big)|_{N^{\epsilon}{\mathcal{X}}}\to{\mathcal{E}}|_{|N^{\epsilon}{\mathcal{X}}|}.

Definition 2.40
.

Let
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
be a Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
is an NC structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
. Let
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
,
h^{\mathcal{E}}
,
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
be respectively a connection, an inner product, an NC structure on
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
.

(1)

g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
is said to be
straightened
along
N{\mathcal{X}}
if for
\epsilon:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
sufficiently small, the normal exponential map
\exp^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
is an isometric open embedding.

(2)

{\mathcal{I}}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
is said to be
straightened
along
N{\mathcal{X}}
if for
\epsilon:{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
sufficiently small,
\exp^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
is an open embedding of NC orbifolds.

(3)

\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
is said to be
straightened
along
N{\mathcal{X}}
if
{\rm par}^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
sends the pullback connection
\pi_{N{\mathcal{X}}}^{*}\nabla^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}}}
to
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
.

(4)

{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
is said to be
straightened
along
N{\mathcal{X}}
with respect to
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
if
{\rm par}^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
sends the pullback
\pi_{N{\mathcal{X}}}^{*}{\mathcal{I}}^{{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}}}
to
{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proposition 2.41
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
be an NC pair. Let
{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a suborbifold with ordinary normal bundle and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}}:={\mathcal{E}}|_{\mathcal{X}}
. Then
({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}})
carries an induced NC structure. Suppose
({\mathcal{X}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\#})
is a straightening of
({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{X}})
. Then there exists a straightening
({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
whose restriction to
{\mathcal{X}}
is
(g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}},{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}})
such that

(1)

The metric
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
is straightened along
N{\mathcal{X}}
.

(2)

The NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
is straightened along
N{\mathcal{X}}
with respect to
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
.

(3)

The connection
\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is straightened along
N{\mathcal{X}}
with respect to
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
.

(4)

The NC structure
{\mathcal{I}}_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
is straightened along
N{\mathcal{X}}
with respect to
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
and
\nabla_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

We only give the proof of the extension of the straightened metric. The extensions of other structures are similar and omitted. Let
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}}
be the straightened metric on
{\mathcal{X}}
. Let
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
be an arbitrary Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{U}}
extending
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}}
, which gives a normal exponential map
\exp^{N{\mathcal{X}}}:N^{\epsilon}{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
. Moreover, choose a connection
\nabla_{0}^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
on the normal bundle
N{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
which is straightened with respect to
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}}
and choose an inner product
h_{0}^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
which is preserved by
\nabla_{0}^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
and which is straightened with respect to
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}}
and
\nabla_{0}^{N{\mathcal{X}}}
(the existence of such a connection and an inner product is guaranteed by Proposition
2.34
). The triple
(g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}},h_{0}^{N{\mathcal{X}}},\nabla_{0}^{N{\mathcal{X}}})
induces a bundle metric
g_{0}^{TN{\mathcal{X}}}
on the total space
N{\mathcal{X}}
, which is a straightened metric. Then using the normal exponential map to pushforward the bundle metric to a tubular neighborhood
|N^{\epsilon}{\mathcal{X}}|
. This gives a straightened metric in
|N^{\epsilon}{\mathcal{X}}|
which extends
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{X}}}
. Then using the relative version of Proposition
2.29
to find a straightened metric
g_{0}^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{U}}
without altering its value near
{\mathcal{X}}
.
∎

2.5.
Normally complex sections

Now we discuss the notion of normally complex sections originally introduced by Parker
[
Par13
]
which generalizes Fukaya–Ono’s notion of normally polynomial sections
[
FO01
]
. Such sections will form the category of perturbations we use.

2.5.1.
Normally complex maps between representations

We first discuss equivariant polynomial maps between representations. Let
G
be a finite group and
V,W
be complex representations of
G
. Let
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,W)
be the space of
G
-equivariant polynomial maps. For
d\geq 0
, let

{\rm Poly}_{G}^{d}(V,W)\subseteq{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,W)

be the subspace of maps of degrees at most
d
. More generally, for an NC triple
(G,V,W)
, define

\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}(V,W):={\rm Poly}_{G}(\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})\oplus W_{G}.

Its elements, typically denoted by
P
, can be viewed as
G
-equivariant maps from
V
to
W
which are independent of the variable
v_{G}\in V_{G}
and whose
W_{G}
-components are constant maps. Hence there is an inclusion

\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}(V,W)\subseteq C_{G}^{\infty}(V,W)

where the latter is the space of smooth
G
-equivariant maps. Let
\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)\subseteq\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}(V,W)
be the subset of maps of degree at most
d
. It is the model of maps considered in
[
FO01
]
. Parker
[
Par13
]
generalizes it to the following larger space of equivariant maps.

Definition 2.42
.

Let
(G,V,W)
be an NC triple. A
G
-equivariant
normally complex map
from
V
to
W
is an element of the module over the ring of
G
-invariant smooth functions
C_{G}^{\infty}(V;{\mathbb{R}})
generated by
\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}(V,W)
, denoted by

C_{G}^{\rm NC}(V,W)\subseteq C_{G}^{\infty}(V,W).

A
lift
of an NC map
S\in C_{G}^{\rm NC}(V,W)
is a
G
-invariant smooth map

{\mathfrak{p}}\in C_{G}^{\infty}(V,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W))

such that
S(v)=\mathfrak{p}(v)(v)
. We denote

S_{\mathfrak{p}}(v)={\mathfrak{p}}(v)(v).

Notice that there is a natural diagram

C_{G}^{\infty}(V,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W))\to\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{d}C_{G}^{\infty}(V,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W))\to C_{G}^{\rm NC}(V,W).

Remark 2.43
.

In general
C_{G}^{\rm NC}(V,W)
is only a
C^{0}
-dense subspace of
C_{G}^{\infty}(V,W)
. For example, when
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and
V\cong W\cong{\mathbb{C}}
is the nontrivial complex representation, the map
z\mapsto\overline{z}
, which is
G
-equivariant, cannot be approximated by an NC map in
C^{1}
-topology.

2.5.2.
Change of symmetry groups

Let
(G,V,W)
be an NC triple. If
H\subseteq G
is a
V
-essential subgroup, then any
G
-equivariant map
f:V\to W
is automatically
H
-equivariant. Hence there is an inclusion

C_{G}^{\infty}(V,W)\hookrightarrow C_{H}^{\infty}(V,W).

Lemma 2.44
.

Under the above inclusion, the space
C_{G}^{\rm NC}(V,W)
is mapped into
C_{H}^{\rm NC}(V,W)
. Moreover, there is a natural map
\mu
(the dashed arrow below) making the following diagram commute.

\textstyle{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{d}C_{G}^{\infty}\left(V,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{C_{G}^{\rm NC}(V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{d}C_{H}^{\infty}\left(V,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}(V,W)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{H}^{\rm NC}(V,W)}

Proof.

A map
{\mathfrak{p}}:V\to\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
is determined by its graph

{\rm graph}({\mathfrak{p}})\subseteq M^{d}(G,V,W):=V\times\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W).

We would like to define a natural map

\mu:M^{d}(G,V,W)\to M^{d}(H,V,W)

which will play an important role. Given
v\in V
and
P\in\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
, with respect to the basic decomposition (for the
H
-action)
V=V_{H}\oplus\check{V}_{H}
, write
v=v_{H}+\check{v}_{H}
; with respect to the basic decomposition
W=W_{H}\oplus\check{W}_{H}
, write
P=P_{H}+\check{P}_{H}
. Then define

\mu(v,P):=(v,\check{P}_{H}(v_{H},\cdot),P_{H}(v))\in M^{d}(H,V,W).

(2.3)

It is an
H
-equivariant map. Then there is a unique
H
-invariant map
{\mathfrak{q}}:V\to\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}(V,W)
whose graph is
\mu({\rm graph}({\mathfrak{p}}))
. By abuse of notation, define

\mu({\mathfrak{p}}):={\mathfrak{q}}.

From the definition one can see that
S_{\mathfrak{q}}=P
as maps from
V
to
W
.
∎

2.5.3.
Family case

We also consider the family case.

Definition 2.45
.

Let
X
be a manifold.

(1)

A
family NC triple
over
X
is a triple
(G,F,E)
consisting of a pair of real vector bundles
\pi_{F}:F\to X
,
\pi_{E}:E\to X
with fiberwise linear
G
-actions, a
G
-invariant complex structure
I^{\check{F}_{G}}
on
\check{F}_{G}\subseteq F
and a
G
-invariant complex structure
I^{\check{E}_{G}}
on
\check{E}_{G}\subseteq E
.

(2)

Given a family NC triple
(G,F,E)
, let
C_{G}^{\infty}(F,E)
be the space of
G
-equivariant smooth bundle maps
S:F\to E
. Let

\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}(F,E)\subseteq C_{G}^{\infty}(F,E)

be the subbundle whose fiber at
x\in X
is the space
\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}(F_{x},E_{x})
, and
\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(F,E)\subseteq\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}(F,E)
be the subbundle of fiberwise polynomial maps of degree at most
d
.

(3)

An element
S\in C_{G}^{\infty}(F,E)
is called a
normally complex
bundle map if there exists
d\geq 0
such that it is an element of the module over
C_{G}^{\infty}(F;{\mathbb{R}})
generated by
\Gamma(\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(F,E))
, the global sections of
\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(F,E)
. Denote

C_{G}^{\rm NC}(F,E):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{d}C_{G}^{\infty}(F;{\mathbb{R}})\cdot\Gamma(\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(F,E))\subseteq C_{G}^{\infty}(F,E).

When we discuss transversality, we need to choose a particular presentation of a NC bundle map. Notice that for each
d\geq 0
, there is a natural map

\displaystyle C_{G}^{\infty}\Big(F,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(F,E)\Big)

\displaystyle\to C_{G}^{\rm NC}(F,E)

(2.4)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}

\displaystyle\mapsto S_{\mathfrak{p}}:=(F\ni v\mapsto{\mathfrak{p}}(v)(v)).

Definition 2.46
.

(cf.
[
Par13
, Definition 4.12]
)
A
lift
of an NC bundle map
S\in C_{G}^{\rm NC}(F,E)
is a
G
-invariant bundle map
{\mathfrak{p}}:F\to\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(F,E)
such that
S=S_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

It is important to consider how the NC bundle maps behave under restriction to a smaller symmetry group. Let
H\subseteq G
be an
F
-essential subgroup. Then there is the natural inclusion

C_{G}^{\infty}(F,E)\to C_{H}^{\infty}(F,E).

(2.5)

Lemma
2.44
implies that (
2.5
) sends
C_{G}^{\rm NC}(F,E)
into
C_{H}^{\rm NC}(F,E)
. Moreover, there is a natural map on the level of lifts.

Lemma 2.47
.

There is a natural arrow
\mu
(the dotted one) making the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{d}C_{G}^{\infty}\Big(F,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(F,E)\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{C_{G}^{\rm NC}(F,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{d}C_{H}^{\infty}\Big(F,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}(F,E)\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{H}^{\rm NC}(F,E)}

Proof.

One simply applies the map
\mu
provided by Lemma
2.44
along each fiber of
F
and the proof follows from the same argument.
∎

One can view the total space
F
as the total space of the pullback bundle
\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H}
. Then an
H
-equivariant bundle map
S:F\to E
can also be viewed as an
H
-equivariant bundle map from
\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H}
to
\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}E
. Hence one has the natural identification

C_{H}^{\infty}(F,E)\cong C_{H}^{\infty}(\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H},\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}E),

(2.6)

which induces the identification

\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}(F,E)\cong\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}(\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H},\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}E).

The following lemma is readily to check.

Lemma 2.48
.

The map (
2.6
) sends
C_{H}^{\rm NC}(F,E)
to
C_{H}^{\rm NC}(\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H},\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}E)
and the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{C_{H}^{\infty}\left(F,\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}(F,E)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{C_{H}^{\rm NC}(F,E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{H}^{\infty}\left(\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H},\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}\left(\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H},\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}E\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{H}^{\rm NC}\left(\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}\check{F}_{H},\pi_{F_{H}}^{*}E\right)}

2.5.4.
Normally complex sections

Now we define the notion of normally complex sections over orbifolds. Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
be an NC pair equipped with a straightening
({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
. Let
\hat{C}=(G,U,E,\hat{\psi})
be a bundle chart. Let
H\subseteq G
be a
U
-essential subgroup. Recall that the straightening induces exponential maps

\exp^{NU_{H}}:N^{\epsilon}U_{H}\to|N^{\epsilon}U_{H}|\subseteq U

and the bundle isomorphism

{\rm par}^{NU_{H}}:\pi_{NU_{H}}^{*}E|_{U_{H}}\cong E|_{|N^{\epsilon}U_{H}|}.

Then any
G
-equivariant section
S:U\to E
induces a
G
-equivariant bundle map

S_{H}^{\#}:N^{\epsilon}U_{H}\to E|_{U_{H}}.

Definition 2.49
.

Under the above setting, let
\hat{C}=(G,U,E,\hat{\psi})
be a bundle chart of
{\mathcal{E}}
and
S:U\to E
is a
G
-equivariant section.

(1)

Let
H\subseteq G
be a
U
-essential subgroup. We say that
S
is
normally complex along
NU_{H}
if for
\epsilon:U_{H}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
sufficiently small, the bundle map
S_{H}^{\#}
is the restriction of an NC bundle map from
NU_{H}
to
E|_{U_{H}}
to
N^{\epsilon}U_{H}
.

(2)

S
is said to be
normally complex
if it is NC along
NU_{H}
for all
U
-essential subgroups
H\subseteq G
.

(3)

A smooth section
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is an
NC section
if its pullback to each bundle chart is an NC section.

The importance of straightening is stated in the following lemma, which guarantees that the compatibility of being normally complex along tubular neighborhoods of fixed point loci of different groups.

Lemma 2.50
.

Let
\hat{C}=(G,U,E,\hat{\psi})
be a bundle chart. Suppose
S:U\to E
is NC along
NU_{G}
, then for any
U
-essential subgroup
H\subseteq G
,
S
is NC along
NU_{H}
in a neighborhood of
U_{G}
.

Proof.

By the definition of straightening, near
U_{G}
, we may regard
U
with a disk bundle of
NU_{G}
equipped with the bundle metric and bundle NC structure, and identify
E
with the pullback bundle
\pi_{NU_{G}}^{*}(E|_{U_{G}})
with the pullback structures. By the definition of NC sections,
S_{G}^{\#}
is the restriction of a section in
C_{G}^{\rm NC}(NU_{G},E|_{U_{G}})
. Let
H\subseteq_{U}G
be an essential subgroup. In the neighborhood
|N^{\epsilon}U_{G}|
for
\epsilon
sufficiently small, the
H
-fixed point set is the total space of
(NU_{G})_{H}
. By Lemma
2.47
, Lemma
2.48
, and the definition of straightenings, the restriction of
S_{H}^{\#}
to a neighborhood of
(NU_{G})_{H}
is also normally complex.
∎

Next we prove some simple formal properties of the space of normally complex sections. Let
\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
be the space of smooth sections of
{\mathcal{E}}
. Let
({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
be a straightening of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
. Let

\Gamma^{\rm NC}({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})\subseteq\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})

be the subset of normally complex sections (with respect to the given straightening).

Lemma 2.51
.

\Gamma^{\rm NC}({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
is a
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{U}})
-submodule of
\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
.

Proof.

It follows from the fact that
C_{G}^{\rm NC}(F,E)
is a
C_{G}^{\infty}(F;{\mathbb{R}})
-submodule (cf. Definition
2.45
).
∎

Lastly we prove the
C^{0}
-density of the space of normally complex sections.

Proposition 2.52
.

\Gamma^{\rm NC}({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
is dense in
\Gamma({\mathcal{E}})
with respect to the
C^{0}
-norm induced by any inner product on
{\mathcal{E}}
.

To prove this result, we first recall a fundamental lemma proved by Fukaya–Ono
[
FO01
]
. For the convenience of the reader we provide the proof here.

Lemma 2.53

(Fukaya–Ono’s lemma)
.

[
FO01
, Lemma 5]

Let
(G,V,W)
be an NC triple. Then for any
d\geq|G|
and any
v_{0}\in V
with isotropy subgroup
H\subseteq G
and
w_{0}\in W_{H}
, there exists
P\in\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
such that
P(v_{0})=w_{0}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that it suffices to consider the case when
V_{G}=W_{G}=\{0\}
; in particular,
V
and
W
are complex representations. Then by decomposing
W
into irreducible components, we may assume that
W
is an irreducible (complex) representation of
G
. Define the
G
-vector space

R:=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in G}{\mathbb{C}}\{\langle\gamma\rangle\}

with
G
-action defined as

g\left(\sum_{\gamma}c_{\gamma}\cdot\langle\gamma\rangle\right)=\sum_{\gamma}c_{\gamma}\cdot\langle\gamma g^{-1}\rangle=\sum_{\gamma}c_{\gamma g}\cdot\langle\gamma\rangle.

Since
R
is a regular representation, there is a
G
-equivariant homomorphism
\Psi:R\to W
and an element
r\in R
such that

\Psi(r)=\Psi\left(\sum_{\gamma}w_{\gamma}\cdot\langle\gamma\rangle\right)=w_{0}.

Since
w\in W_{H}
, for all
h\in H
, one has

\Psi(hr)=h\Psi(r)=w_{0}.

Hence by taking average over
H
, one may assume that

\gamma^{\prime}H=\gamma^{\prime\prime}H\Longrightarrow w_{\gamma^{\prime}}=w_{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}.

Now we claim that one can choose a complex polynomial
f:V\to{\mathbb{C}}
(not necessarily
G
-invariant) of degree at most
|G|
such that

\forall\gamma\in G,\ f(\gamma v_{0})=w_{\gamma}.

Indeed, there are
n:=|G/H|
distinct elements in the
G
-orbit of
v_{0}
. One can choose a linear decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
such that
V_{1}
is one-dimensional and that the projection of these
n
distinct elements are still distinct in
V_{1}
. Then by Lagrange’s method of interpolation, one can find a complex polynomial
f:V_{1}\to{\mathbb{C}}
of degree at most
|G|
taking the prescribed values
w_{\gamma}
at the corresponding projection image of
\gamma v_{0}
in
V_{1}
. Extend
f
trivially to
V
one obtains a polynomial
f:V\to{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying the required conditions. Now define
P:V\to W
by

P(v)=\Psi\left(\sum_{\gamma\in G}f(\gamma v)\cdot\langle\gamma\rangle\right).

Then this is a
G
-equivariant polynomial map sending
v_{0}
to
w_{0}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.52
.

Fix an inner product on
{\mathcal{E}}
. Given
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\in\Gamma({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
and
\delta>0
, it suffices to find a section
{\mathcal{S}}\in\Gamma^{\rm NC}({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
such that
\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\delta
. We construct
{\mathcal{S}}
by local approximations and gluing using a partition of unity.

For each
p\in{\mathcal{U}}
, one can find a bundle chart
\hat{C}=(G,U,E,\hat{\psi})
centered at
p
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

The normal bundle
NU_{G}\to U_{G}
is trivial with fiber being a representation
V
of
G
. The tubular neighborhood
|N^{\epsilon}U_{G}|
is the whole chart
U
, identified with a subset of
U_{G}\times V
.

(2)

E|_{U_{G}}
is trivial with fiber being an NC representation
W
of
G
. Then the straightening induces a trivialization
E\cong U\times W
and the section
S_{0}:U\to E
is equivalent to a
G
-equivariant map
S_{0}:U\to W
.

Claim.
For
d\geq|G|
and any

\epsilon>0
there is a
G
-invariant map

{\mathfrak{p}}:U_{G}\times V\to\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)

providing a lift of an NC map
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that

\sup_{(y,v)\in U}\|S_{0}(y,v)-S_{\mathfrak{p}}(y,v)\|\leq\delta.

Assuming this claim is true, we prove the proposition as follows. As
{\mathcal{U}}
is second-countable, it is Lindelöf, hence there is a countable collection of points
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{U}}
and bundle charts
\hat{C}_{i}=(G_{i},U_{i},E_{i},\hat{\psi}_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots
, which satisfy the above conditions such that the collection
\psi_{i}(U_{i})
cover
{\mathcal{U}}
. Let
S_{0,i}:U_{i}\to E_{i}
be the pullback of
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}
to the chart
\hat{C}_{i}
. The above claim implies that there exists a
G_{i}
-equivariant NC section
S_{i}:U_{i}\to E_{i}
such that

\|S_{0,i}-S_{i}\|_{C^{0}(U_{i})}\leq\frac{\delta}{2^{i}}.

Then choose a smooth partition of unity
\rho_{i}
subordinate to the open cover
\psi_{i}(U_{i})
. Consider

{\mathcal{S}}_{i}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}S_{i}

which belongs to
\Gamma^{\rm NC}({\mathcal{U}}^{\#},{\mathcal{E}}^{\#})
by Lemma
2.51
. Then one has

\|{\mathcal{S}}_{0}-{\mathcal{S}}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\|\rho_{i}{\mathcal{S}}_{0,i}-\rho_{i}{\mathcal{S}}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\|S_{0,i}-S_{i}\|_{C^{0}(U_{i})}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta}{2^{i}}=\delta.

Now we prove the above claim. For each
x=(y_{0},v_{0})\in U
, the equivariance condition on
S_{0}
implies
S_{0}(x)\in W_{G_{x}}\subseteq W
. Then by Lemma
2.53
, for
d\geq|G|
, there exists
P_{x}\in\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
such that
P_{x}(v_{0})=S_{0}(x)
. We view
P_{x}
as a map

P_{x}:U\to W,\ P_{x}(y,v)=P_{x}(v),

which is a
G
-equivariant NC section of
E\to U
. Then for the given
\delta>0
, one can find an open neighborhood
O_{x}\subseteq U
of
x
such that

\sup_{x^{\prime}\in O_{x}}|S_{0}(x)-S_{0}(x^{\prime})|+\sup_{x^{\prime}\in O_{x}}|P_{x}(x)-P_{x}(x^{\prime})|\leq\delta.

As
U
is Lindelöf and paracompact, one can find countably many points
x_{j}
such that the collection
O_{x_{j}}
is a locally finite cover of
U
. Choose a partition of unity
\rho_{j}
on
U
subordinate to
O_{x_{j}}
. Define

{\mathfrak{q}}:U\to\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W),\ {\mathfrak{q}}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\rho_{j}(x)P_{x_{j}}.

We first check that this (not necessarily
G
-invariant) map defines a section that is close to the original section
S_{0}
. Indeed, for each
x=(y,v)\in U
, then one has

|S_{0}(x)-{\mathfrak{q}}(x)(v)|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\rho_{j}(x)|S_{0}(x)-P_{x_{j}}(x)|.

For
j=1,\ldots,l
, if
x\notin O_{x_{j}}
, then
\rho_{j}(x)=0
and the corresponding summand above vanishes; if
x\in O_{x_{j}}
, then

|S_{0}(x)-P_{x_{j}}(x)|\leq|S_{0}(x)-S_{0}(x_{j})|+|P_{x_{j}}(x)-P_{x_{j}}(x_{j})|\leq\delta.

Hence one has

\sup_{x\in U}|S_{0}(x)-{\mathfrak{q}}(x)(v)|\leq\delta.

Now we make
{\mathfrak{q}}
invariant by setting

{\mathfrak{p}}(x)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\tau\in G}{\mathfrak{q}}(\tau x)\in\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W).

Then
{\mathfrak{p}}
satisfies the condition stated in the above claim.
∎

3.
Whitney Stratifications on the Variety
Z

This section serves as another facet of the technical foundation of the FOP transversality condition, which depends on an intricate analysis of a special class of affine varieties, especially their Whitney stratifications. Many technical discussions we carry out here originated from Parker’s work
[
Par13
]
.

Before the technical discussion, we explain why such study of Whitney stratifications is necessary. As mentioned in the introduction, by doing single-valued perturbation to a section of an orbifold vector bundle, the best hope is to have a stratified zero locus whose isotropy free part has codimension two or higher frontier. The local models for such a stratified zero locus are certain “universal” zero locus, i.e., the varieties labelled by
Z
. The Whitney stratification on
Z
we specify in this section will give models for stratifying the perturbed zero locus. The challenge in the discussion is that one needs to identify a kind of canonical Whitney stratifications which have sufficient functorial properties so that the corresponding transversality condition is well-defined and is an open condition on an orbifold. One is then compelled to revisit arguments of Whitney and Mather etc. about the existence and properties of canonical Whitney stratifications on algebraic varieties and adopt them to the current situation.

In Subsection
3.1
we review basics about Whitney stratifications. Some technical results stated here are proved in Appendix
B
. In Subsection
3.2
we specify the consideration to a special class of algebraic varieties whose Whitney stratifications are essential for defining the FOP transversality condition. The proofs of several important propositions are given at the end of this section. In Subsection
3.3
, we describe a universal set which will index the strata of the zero loci of FOP transverse sections. The rest of this section contains proofs of results stated in Subsection
3.2
.

3.1.
Basic notions and facts about Whitney stratifications

We first recall basic notions and facts about Whitney stratifications. Proofs of some results will be given in Appendix
B
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold.

(1)

Let
X,Y\subseteq M
be disjoint smooth submanifolds. We say that
(X,Y)
satisfies
Whitney’s condition (b)
at
y\in Y
if the following is true. Given a sequence
x_{i}\in X
converging to
y
and
y_{i}\in Y
converging to
y
,
suppose the secant line
\overline{x_{i}y_{i}}

7
7

7

The secant line depends on a choice of local coordinate system near
y
, but the condition does not depend on such choices.
converges to a line
L_{y}\subseteq T_{y}M
and
T_{x_{i}}X
converges to a
{\rm dim}X
-dimensional subspace
H_{y}\subseteq T_{y}M
, then
L_{y}\subseteq H_{y}
. We say that
(X,Y)
is
Whitney regular
if
(X,Y)
satisfies Whitney’s condition (b) at all points
y\in Y
.

(2)

A stratification (Definition
2.11
) of a subset
Z\subseteq M
, denoted by
{\mathfrak{Z}}=\{Z_{\lambda}\}
, is called a
Whitney stratification
if all strata are smooth submanifolds and each distinct pair
(Z_{\lambda},Z_{\mu})
of strata is Whitney regular.

(3)

Let
N
be a smooth manifold and
f:N\to M
be a smooth map. For a Whitney stratified subset
Z\subseteq M
,
f
is said to be
transverse
to
Z
with respect to a Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}
on
Z
, if
f
is transverse to each stratum of
{\mathfrak{Z}}
. The
naive pullback Whitney stratification
on
f^{-1}(Z)
is the stratification

f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}:=\Big\{f^{-1}(Z_{\alpha})\neq\emptyset\ |\ Z_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}\Big\}

(see Lemma
B.2
proving that this partition satisfies the axiom of frontier). Notice that there is a canonical map between the sets of strata:

f_{*}:f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}\to{\mathfrak{Z}}.

In particular, for any open subset
U\subseteq M
, the
restriction
of
{\mathfrak{Z}}
to
U\cap Z
is the pullback of
{\mathfrak{Z}}
by the natural inclusion
\iota:U\hookrightarrow M
, denoted by
{\mathfrak{Z}}|_{U\cap Z}
.

(4)

Suppose
f:N\to M
is transverse to
{\mathfrak{Z}}
as above. The
refined pullback
of
{\mathfrak{Z}}
is the Whitney stratification (cf. Lemma
B.3
) on
f^{-1}(Z)

f^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{Z}}:=\Big\{\ {\rm connected\ components\ of\ }f^{-1}(Z_{\lambda})\ |\ Z_{\lambda}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}\Big\}.

In particular, denote by
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\dagger}
be the refined pullback by the identity map; in other words,
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\dagger}
is obtained from
{\mathfrak{Z}}
by taking connected components. Notice that there is canonically induced order-preserving map

f_{\dagger}:f^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{Z}}\to{\mathfrak{Z}}.

Whintey stratification may not exist on an arbitrary subset. It was originally proved by Whitney
[
Whi65
]
in the complex analytic category that analytic sets have Whitney stratifications. We make a few more definitions.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
M
be a complex manifold.

(1)

A subset
U\subseteq M
is called a
strongly analytic subset
if both
\overline{U}
and
\overline{U}\setminus U
are closed analytic subsets.
U
is called a
strongly analytic submanifold
if it is an analytic submanifold and a strongly analytic set.

(2)

A Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}
on a subset
Z\subseteq M
is called
strongly analytic
if its strata are all strongly analytic submanifolds.

Whitney’s construction actually provides an “optimal” Whitney stratification. Below we define how to compare different Whitney stratifications.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
M
be an
m
-dimensional manifold and
Z\subseteq M
.

(1)

The
dimension filtration
of a Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}=\{Z_{\lambda}\}
on
Z
is

Z={\mathfrak{Z}}_{m}\supseteq{\mathfrak{Z}}_{m-1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq{\mathfrak{Z}}_{0}

where

{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}:=\bigcup_{{\rm dim}(Z_{\lambda})\leq k}Z_{\lambda}.

(2)

Two Whitney stratifications
{\mathfrak{Z}}
and
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
on
Z
are called
equivalent
, denoted by
{\mathfrak{Z}}\equiv{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
, if their dimension filtrations coincide. We write
{\mathfrak{Z}}<{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
if there exists
k
such that

\displaystyle\ {\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}={\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime}\ \forall l>k,\

\displaystyle\ {\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}\subsetneq{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime}.

We write
{\mathfrak{Z}}\leq{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
if either
{\mathfrak{Z}}\equiv{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
or
{\mathfrak{Z}}<{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z)
be the set of all smooth Whitney stratifications on
Z
and let
{\mathcal{N}}(Z)\subseteq\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z)
be a subset. We say
{\mathfrak{Z}}\in{\mathcal{N}}(Z)
is
minimal
within
{\mathcal{N}}(Z)
if for any
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(Z)
, either
{\mathfrak{Z}}\equiv{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
or
{\mathfrak{Z}}<{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
.

It is easy to see that if a minimal Whitney stratification exists, then it is unique up to equivalence.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold equipped with a stratification
{\mathfrak{M}}=\{M_{\alpha}\}
where each stratum
M_{\alpha}
is a locally closed smooth submanifold (
{\mathfrak{M}}
itself may not be a Whitney stratification). We say a Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}
on a subset
Z\subseteq M

respects
{\mathfrak{M}}
if each
Z\cap M_{\alpha}
is a union of strata in
{\mathfrak{Z}}
. Denote by

\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z;{\mathfrak{M}})\subseteq\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z)

the subset of
{\mathfrak{M}}
-respecting Whitney stratifications on
Z
.

The case we are interested in comes from group actions.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
G
be a finite group acting on a smooth manifold
M
. The
action stratification
on
M
is the stratification
{\mathfrak{M}}
indexed by
M
-essential subgroups of
G
whose strata are

M_{H}^{*}:=\Big\{x\in M\ |\ G_{x}=H\Big\}.

For each
G
-invariant subset
Z\subseteq M
, let

\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z;G)\subseteq\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z)

be the subset of Whitney stratifications which respects the action stratification.

In the appendix we prove the following theorem, which can be viewed as a refinement of Whintey’s original theorem.

Proposition 3.6
.

In the situation of Definition
3.4
, suppose
M
is a complex manifold, each
M_{\alpha}
is a strongly analytic submanifold, and
Z\subseteq M
is a closed analytic subset. Then there exists a unique minimal element
{\mathfrak{Z}}\in\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z;{\mathfrak{M}})
that has connected strata. In addition,
{\mathfrak{Z}}
satisfies the following properties.

(1)

For each open subset
U\subseteq M
,
{\mathfrak{Z}}|_{U\cap Z}
(which may not have connected strata) is minimal in
\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(U\cap Z,{\mathfrak{M}}|_{U})
.

(2)

{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a strongly analytic Whitney stratification (Definition
3.2
).

(3)

If
f:M\to M
is a diffeomorphism such that
f^{*}{\mathfrak{M}}={\mathfrak{M}}
and
f(Z)=Z
, then
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}={\mathfrak{Z}}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.4
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

The construction needed for the proof was provided in the original paper of Whitney (see
[
Whi65
, Section 18–21]
). However, as Whitney’s proof does not explicitly discuss the minimality (which is important for our purpose), we need to revisit his argument and reproduce the proof in Appendix
B
.

For certain applications (for example when proving the Arnold conjecture over
{\mathbb{Z}}
in
[
BX22
]
), we need the property that the certain Whitney stratifications respect direct product. Notice that products of Whitney stratifications are still Whitney stratifications (see
[
GWdPL76
, (1.2)]
). In the appendix we also prove the following.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
M
resp.
N
be complex manifolds equipped with stratifications
{\mathfrak{M}}
resp.
{\mathfrak{N}}
by strongly analytic submanifolds. Let
S\subseteq M
resp.
T\subseteq N
be closed analytic sets. Let
{\mathfrak{S}}
resp.
{\mathfrak{T}}
be the minimal
{\mathfrak{M}}
-respecting resp.
{\mathfrak{N}}
-respecting Whitney stratification on
S
resp.
T
provided by Proposition
3.6
. Then
{\mathfrak{S}}\times{\mathfrak{T}}
is a minimal
{\mathfrak{M}}\times{\mathfrak{N}}
-respecting Whitney stratification on
S\times T
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B.5
.
∎

3.2.
The canonical Whitney stratifications on the variety
Z

Now we use the existence results (Theorem
3.6
) to specify the canonical Whitney stratifications on the class of algebraic varieties
Z
. We then state the properties of such Whitney stratifications which will be proved later in this section.

3.2.1.
The variety
Z

Recall that for each NC triple
(G,V,W)
(Definition
2.15
) and
d\geq 0
, there is the vector space introduced in the proof of Lemma
2.44

M^{d}:=M^{d}(G,V,W)=V\times\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)

where
G
acts on the factor
V
. There is the natural
evaluation map

{\rm ev}:M^{d}\to W,\ (v,P)\mapsto P(v)\in W

whose zero locus is a
G
-invariant subvariety

Z^{d}=Z^{d}(G,V,W):=\Big\{(v,P)\in M^{d}(G,V,W)\ |\ {\rm ev}(v,P)=0\Big\}.

In particular, the
W_{G}
-component of
P
for a point in
Z^{d}
is
0
. The group
G
acts on the factor
V
and
Z^{d}
is
G
-invariant. Hence there is an action stratification on
M^{d}
(Definition
3.5
). The strata are indxed by
V
-essential subgroups
H\subseteq_{V}W
with

\displaystyle\ V_{H}^{*}=\{v\in V_{H}\ |\ G_{v}=H\},\

\displaystyle\ M^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}=V_{H}^{*}\times\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W).

For each
d\geq 0
, denote

Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}:=Z^{d}(G,V,W)\cap M^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}.

We first look at this induced partition on
Z^{d}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

For all
d\geq|G|
and
H\subseteq_{V}G
,
Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}
is a smooth submanifold of dimension
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W))+{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(V_{H})-{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{H})
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Fukaya–Ono’s lemma (Lemma
2.53
). For each
H\subseteq_{V}G
and
P\in\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
, with respect to the basic decomposition
W=\check{W}_{H}\oplus W_{H}
, we can write
P=\check{P}_{H}+P_{H}
. Then the
H
-equivariance of
P
implies that

\check{P}_{H}|_{V_{H}}\equiv 0.

Then

Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}=\Big\{(v_{H},P)\in V_{H}^{*}\times\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)\ |\ P_{H}(v_{H})=0\Big\}.

Then Lemma
2.53
implies that when
d\geq|G|
,
Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}
is transversely cut out, having dimension being
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W))+{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(V_{H})-{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{H})
. Indeed, the linearization of the defining equation of
Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}
is surjective: given
(v_{H},P)\in Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{*}
, Lemma
2.53
ensures that for any
w_{0}\in W_{H}
, we can find a polynomial
P
such that
P(v_{H})=w_{0}
, and we can linearize the equation
P_{H}(v_{H})=0
along the direction speficied by
P
along the polynomial component. So, the smoothness follows accordingly, and the dimension formula follows by a count of number of parameters.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

Proposition
3.9
implies that
Z^{d}(G,V,W)
has a natural partition into smooth manifolds. However, this partition is not a stratification, as the axiom of frontier fails (see Definition
A.1
).

3.2.2.
The canonical Whitney stratification

Recall that for the NC pair
(G,V,W)
, one has the basic decompositions

\displaystyle\ V=V_{G}\oplus\check{V}_{G},\

\displaystyle\ W=W_{G}\oplus\check{W}_{G}.

Moreover, recall the definition

\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)={\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})\times W_{G}.

Then

M^{d}=V_{G}\times M^{d}(G,\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})\times W_{G}

where
\check{M}^{d}:=M^{d}(G,\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})
is a complex vector space and

Z^{d}=V_{G}\times Z^{d}(G,\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})\times\{0\}

where
\check{Z}^{d}:=Z^{d}(G,\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})\subseteq\check{M}^{d}
is a complex algebraic variety with a
G
-action. In this case, by Proposition
3.6
, there exists a unique minimal element

\check{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}\in\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(\check{Z}^{d};G)

with connected strata. Using the identification
Z^{d}=V_{G}\times\check{Z}^{d}\times\{0\}
, there is a canonically induced Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}
on
Z^{d}
given by

{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}=\Big\{V_{G}\times\check{Z}_{\alpha}^{d}\times\{0\}\ |\ \check{Z}^{d}_{\alpha}\in\check{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}\Big\}

It is easy to check that
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}
is a minimal Whitney stratification in
\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z^{d};G)
. We call
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}
the
canonical Whitney stratification
on
Z^{d}
.

Example 3.11
.

Consider the situation where
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
,
V\cong{\mathbb{C}}
is the weight 1 representation and
W\cong{\mathbb{C}}
is the weight 2 representation. Let
d=5
. Then

{\rm Poly}{}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}}^{5}(V,W)=\{P(v)=av^{2}+bv^{5}\ |\ a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}\}

and

Z^{5}=\{(a,b,v)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{3}\ |\ av^{2}+bv^{5}=0\}.

The canonical Whitney stratification has three strata. Two top strata are

{\mathfrak{Z}}^{5}_{\lambda_{1}}=\{(a,b,v)\ |\ v\neq 0,\ a+bv^{3}=0\}

and

{\mathfrak{Z}}^{5}_{\lambda_{2}}=\{(a,b,v)\ |\ v=0,\ a\neq 0\}.

The intersection of their closure is the lowest stratum

{\mathfrak{Z}}^{5}_{\lambda_{3}}=\{(a,b,v)\ |\ v=0,a=0\}.

Notice that this Whitney stratification is the same as the minimal Whitney stratification of
Z^{5}
without requiring to respect the action stratification.

Example 3.12
.

Consider
G={\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
and
R\cong{\mathbb{C}}
being the weight 1 representation. Choose
V=R\oplus R
,
W=R
, and
d=1
. Let the variable of
V
be
(x,y)
. Then

{\rm Poly}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}}^{1}(V,W)=\{P(x,y)=ax+by\ |\ a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}\}

and
Z^{1}
is the cone
ax+by=0
. It only singularity is the origin and its minimial Whitney stratification (without referring to the group action) has two strata: the smooth locus and the cone point. However, with respect to the group action, the canonical Whitney stratification is finer: its top stratum is

{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\lambda_{1}}^{1}=\{(a,b,x,y)\ |\ (x,y)\neq(0,0),\ ax+by=0\}

which is properly contained in the smooth locus of
Z^{1}
.

The various properties of the FOP transversality condition listed in Theorem
1.1
relies on the following important properties of the canonical Whitney stratification.

3.2.3.
Invariance

Proposition 3.13
.

Given an isomorphism
(G,V,W)\cong(G^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime})
of NC triples which induces a linear isomorphism
f:M^{d}(G,V,W)\to M^{d}(G^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime})
which sends
Z^{d}(G,V,W)
to
Z^{d}(G^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime})
, there holds

f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime})={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W).

In particular,
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)
is
{\rm Aut}(G,V,W)
-invariant, where
{\rm Aut}(G,V,W)
denotes the group consisting of a pair of
G
-equivariant linear isomorphisms of
V
and
W
.

Proof.

It is equivalent to consider the case when
(G,V,W)=(G^{\prime},V^{\prime},W^{\prime})
and
f
is induced from a pair of
G
-equivariant automorphisms
f_{V}:V\to V
and
f_{W}:W\to W
. Then
f
induces a complex linear automorphism of the subspace
M^{d}(G,\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})
preserving the complex variety
Z^{d}(G,\check{V}_{G},\check{W}_{G})
as well as the action stratification and
\check{Z}^{d}
. Then by Proposition
3.6
,
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}
.
∎

Proposition
3.13
allows us to extend the canonical Whitney stratification to the family case. Let
(G,F,E)
be a family NC triple over a manifold
X
(Definition
2.45
). The one can define the vector bundle

M^{d}(G,F,E)\to X

and the
G
-invariant subbundle

Z^{d}(G,F,E)\subseteq M^{d}(G,F,E).

As the structure group of
M^{d}(G,F,E)
is contained in
{\rm Aut}(G,V,W)
, by Proposition
3.13
, there is a locally trivial Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,F,E)
on
Z^{d}(G,F,E)
, which is still called the
canonical Whitney stratification
on
Z^{d}(G,F,E)
.

3.2.4.
Change of degrees

The following proposition is necessary for showing the FOP transversality condition is well-defined and independent of the cut-off degree
d
. Consider
d\geq 0
and
d^{\prime}>d
. Then there is the natural inclusion map

\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}:M^{d}(G,V,W)\to M^{d^{\prime}}(G,V,W),\ (v,P)\mapsto(v,P)

(3.1)

which respects the action stratifications such that
\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}^{-1}(Z^{d^{\prime}})=Z^{d}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

For any NC triple
(G,V,W)
, there exists
d_{1}=d_{1}(G,V,W)\geq|G|
such that when
d>d^{\prime}\geq d_{1}
, there holds

\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}.

Moreover, the corresponding map between the sets of strata

(\sigma_{d^{\prime}d})_{*}:{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}\to{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}

is bijective.

Proof.

See Subsection
3.4
.
∎

3.2.5.
Change of groups

The following proposition is necessary for showing the FOP transversality condition is an open condition on orbifolds. It is also the hardest one to prove. We first recall and introduce some notations. Let
(G,V,W)
be an NC triple and
H\subseteq_{V}G
be an essential subgroup. Using the basic decomposition
V=V_{H}\oplus\check{V}_{H}
, a point of
V
is denoted by
(v_{H},\check{v}_{H})
. Given an element
P\in\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
viewed as a function from
V
to
W
, using the basic decomposition
W=\check{W}_{H}\oplus W_{H}
, we also write

P=\check{P}_{H}+P_{H}.

Then one has defined the map in the proof of Lemma
2.44

\begin{split}\mu:M^{d}(G,V,W)&\ \to M^{d}(H,V,W)\\
(v,P)&\ \mapsto\Big(v,\check{P}_{H}(v_{H},\cdot)+P_{H}(v)\Big).\end{split}

(3.2)

Here the term
P_{H}(v)
also contains the constant map component with value in
W_{G}
. Notice that
\mu
maps
Z^{d}(G,V,W)
into
Z^{d}(H,V,W)
. We shall not expect that the pullback Whitney stratification by
\mu
coincides with
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)
, because the weaker symmetry required by the subgroup
H
may not see some deeper strata of
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)
. However, they do coincide when restricted to the open subset of points whose isotropy group under the
G
-action is a subgroup of
H
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Given an NC triple
(G,V,W)
, there exists
d_{2}\geq d_{1}
such that for all
d\geq d_{2}
the following is true. For each
H\subseteq_{V}G
, consider the open subset

V_{H}^{+}:=\{v\in V\ |\ G_{v}\subseteq H\}\subseteq V.

Denote

\displaystyle\ M^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{+}:=V_{H}^{+}\times\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W),\

\displaystyle\ M^{d}(H,V,W)_{H}^{+}:=V_{H}^{+}\times\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{H}^{d}(V,W)

Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{+}:=Z^{d}(G,V,W)\cap M^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{+}.

Then
\mu|_{M^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{+}}
is transverse to
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(H,V,W)
and

\Big(\mu^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(H,V,W)\Big)|_{Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{+}}={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)|_{Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{+}}.

Moreover, the induced maps between the sets of strata

\mu_{*}:{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)|_{Z^{d}(G,V,W)_{H}^{+}}\to{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(H,V,W)

is invertible and makes the following diagram commutes (when
d^{\prime}\geq d
).

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(H,V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mu_{*})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma_{d^{\prime}d})_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma_{d^{\prime}d})_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}(H,V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mu_{*})^{-1}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}(G,V,W)}

(3.3)

Proof.

See Subsection
3.7
.
∎

3.2.6.
Product

Another property of the canonical Whitney stratification is that it respects direct product. This property is responsible for the
Product Property
stated in Theorem
1.1
which is important in proving the integral Arnold conjecture in
[
BX22
]
and in other potential chain-level applications.

For
i=1,2
, let
(G_{i},V_{i},W_{i})
be NC triples and denote

(G,V,W)=(G_{1}\times G_{2},V_{1}\oplus V_{2},W_{1}\oplus W_{2}).

Proposition 3.16
.

For
d
sufficiently large, consider the map

\begin{split}\xi:M^{d}(G_{1},V_{1},W_{1})\times M^{d}(G_{2},V_{2},W_{2})&\to M^{d}(G,V,W)\\
(v_{1},P_{1},v_{2},P_{2})&\mapsto\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{array}\right],\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{1}&0\\
0&P_{2}\end{array}\right]\right).\end{split}

Then
\xi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G_{1},V_{1},W_{2})\times{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G_{2},V_{2},W_{2})
.

Proof.

See Subsection
3.5
.
∎

3.2.7.
Stabilization property

The following proposition is responsible for the
Stabilization Property
of Theorem
1.1
. We prove the following proposition.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
(G,V,W)
be an NC triple and
R
be an NC representation. For any
d\geq 1
, consider the map

\begin{split}\eta:M^{d}(G,V,W)&\to M^{d}(G,V\oplus R,W\oplus R)\\
(v,P)&\mapsto\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{c}v\\
0\end{array}\right],\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}P&0\\
0&{\rm proj}_{\check{R}_{G}}\end{array}\right]\right)\end{split}

(3.4)

where
{\rm proj}_{\check{R}_{G}}:R\to\check{R}_{G}
is the canonical projections induced from the basic decomposition
R=R_{G}\oplus{\check{R}_{G}}
. Then for
d\geq d_{1}
(given by Proposition
3.14
),
\eta
is transverse to
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V\oplus R,W\oplus R)
and

\eta^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V\oplus R,W\oplus R)={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)

(where
\eta^{\dagger}
is the refined pullback, see Definition
3.1
). Moreover, the induced map

\eta_{\dagger}:{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)\to{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V\oplus R,W\oplus R)

makes the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma_{d^{\prime}d})_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V\oplus R,W\oplus R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\sigma_{d^{\prime}d})_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}(G,V,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\dagger}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}(G,V\oplus R,W\oplus R)}

(3.5)

Proof.

See Subsection
3.6
.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

Proposition
3.14
—
3.17
are stated for general NC triples
(G,V,W)
. However, it is very easy to reduce the proofs to the case when
V_{G}=W_{G}=\{0\}
; in particular,
V
and
W
are complex representations. In the subsections proving these propositions, we always make this simplifying assumption.

3.3.
The set of universal strata

Now we use the previous technical results about the Whitney stratifications on the various varieties
Z^{d}(G,V,W)
to define a set of universal strata
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\rm univ}
whose elements can label the strata of the zero locus of an FOP transverse section (see Theorem
1.1
). Roughly, we define
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\rm univ}
by taking the disjoint union of
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)
for all NC triples of virtual dimension
k
modulo a certain equivalence relation. To make this idea more precise, we need to specify a certain category and define
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\rm univ}
as a colimit.

3.3.1.
Category of NC triples

Definition 3.19
.

(1)

A
morphism of NC triples
from
(H,V,W)
to
(G,X,Y)
is a finite composition of the following two types of morphisms

(a)

A
stabilization
consists of a group isomorphism
H\cong G
, an NC representation
R
of
G
, and a pair of isomorphisms
X\cong V\oplus R
and
Y\cong W\oplus R
of NC representations of
G
.

(b)

An
embedding
consists of a group embedding
H\hookrightarrow G
and a pair of isomorphisms
V\cong X
and
W\cong Y
of NC representations of
H
.

(2)

Two NC triples
(H,V,W)
and
(G,X,Y)
are called
stably equivalent
if they admit stabilizations that are isomorphic (isomorphism of groups + induced isomorphisms of representations). A stable equivalence class is called a
stable NC isotropy type
. The set of stable NC isotropy types is denoted by
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}^{\rm NC}
. For each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}^{\rm NC}_{k}\subseteq\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}^{\rm NC}
be the subset of stable NC isotropy types with virtual dimension
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(V)-{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(W)=k
.

(3)

Define a partial order in
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
as follows. We denote
\underline{\smash{\gamma}}\leq\underline{\smash{\delta}}
if there exist a representative
(H,V,W)
of
\underline{\smash{\gamma}}
, a representative
(G,X,Y)
of
\underline{\smash{\delta}}
, and an embedding from
(H,V,W)
to
(G,X,Y)
.

Notice that there is a natural poset map
{\mathbb{\Gamma}}_{k}^{\rm NC}\to\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
. For each NC pair
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
of orbifolds and vector bundles whose virtual dimension is
k
and a stable NC isotropy triple
\underline{\smash{\gamma}}\in\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
, define

{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{\smash{\gamma}}}:=\bigcup_{\gamma\mapsto\underline{\smash{\gamma}}}{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}

where we take the (disjoint) union of all isotropy types
\gamma
which are sent to
\underline{\smash{\gamma}}
.

3.3.2.
A particular category

The above technical results on Whitney stratifications allow us to gather all strata for different isotropy types. For our purpose related to Theorem
1.1
, we first consider a particular category, denoted by

\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}.

Its objects are triples
({\mathfrak{Z}},\rho,n)
where
{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a partially ordered set,
\rho:{\mathfrak{Z}}\to\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}^{\rm NC}_{k}
is a monotone map, and
n:{\mathfrak{Z}}\to 2{\mathbb{Z}}
is a strictly monotone map. A morphism from
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{1},\rho_{1},n_{1})
to
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{2},\rho_{2},n_{2})
is a strictly monotone poset map
\iota_{21}:{\mathfrak{Z}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{Z}}_{2}
such that
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}\circ\iota_{21}
and
n_{1}=n_{2}\circ\iota_{21}
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Colimits exist in
\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
.

Proof.

Let
\underline{\smash{\bf Poset}}
be the category of posets with morphisms being poset maps and let
\underline{\smash{\bf Poset}}^{s}
be the category of posets with morphisms being strictly monotone poset maps. In both categories colimits exist. On the other hand, there are forgetful functors

\textstyle{\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\underline{\smash{\bf Poset}}^{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\underline{\smash{\bf Poset}}}

where
\pi
takes a triple to its underlying poset. Let
{\bf F}:\underline{\smash{\bf A}}\to\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
be a diagram (functor) where
\underline{\smash{\bf A}}
is any category. Then one obtains a colimit

\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{a}\pi({\bf F}(a))\in{\rm Ob}\underline{\smash{\bf Poset}}^{s}.

As the maps
n:{\mathfrak{Z}}\to 2{\mathbb{Z}}
is a morphism of
\underline{\smash{\bf Poset}}^{s}
, it follows that
n
descends to a strictly monotone map

n:\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{a}\pi({\bf F}(a))\to 2{\mathbb{Z}}.

Moreover,
\iota(\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{a}\pi({\bf F}(a)))
is a colimit of the induced diagram
\underline{\smash{\bf A}}\to\underline{\smash{\bf Poset}}
, hence there exists a natural map

\rho:\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{a}\pi({\bf F}(a))\to\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}^{\rm NC}_{k}.

It is straightforward to check that
(\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{a}\pi({\bf F}(a)),n,\rho)
is a colimit of
{\bf F}(a)
.
∎

3.3.3.
The universal strata

The sets of strata of the canonical Whitney stratifications we considered give rise to objects of the category
\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
. Let
(G,V,W)
be an NC triple of virtual dimension
k
. Define a map

\rho_{k}:{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)\to\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}^{\rm NC}_{k}

as follows: if a stratum
Z^{d}_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)
is contained in
V_{H}^{*}\times\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
for some
V
-essential subgroup
H
, then define
\rho(Z^{d}_{\alpha})
to be the isotropy type represented by the triple
(H,V,W)
. On the other hand, define a map

n_{k}:{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)\to 2{\mathbb{Z}}

which assigns to a stratum
Z^{d}_{\alpha}
the number

n_{k}(Z^{d}_{\alpha}):={\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(Z^{d}_{\alpha})-{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W))-k\in 2{\mathbb{Z}}.

(3.6)

Then
({\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W),\rho_{k},n_{k})
is an object of
\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
. Note that Proposition
3.14
allows us to relate this set for different
d
. Indeed, the bijective map (for
d<d^{\prime}
) induces a morphism

\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}\in{\rm Hom}_{\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}}\left({\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W),{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}(G,V,W)\right).

Then define the colimit

{\mathfrak{Z}}(G,V,W):=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{d}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,V,W)\in\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}.

We would like to make it a functor from the category of NC triples to
\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
.

Lemma 3.21
.

Suppose
(H,V,W)\to(G,X,Y)
is a stabilization morphism. Then there is a canonical map

\eta:{\mathfrak{Z}}(H,V,W)\to{\mathfrak{Z}}(G,X,Y).

Proof.

We identify
H
with
G
,
V
with a subspace of
X
, and
W
with a subspace of
Y
. By definition, there exist a representation
R
of
G
and an equivariant decomposition

X\cong V\oplus R,Y\cong W\oplus R.

By Proposition
3.17
, each such decomposition induces a map

\eta_{\dagger}:{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(H,V,W)\to{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}(G,X,Y)

which is compatible with the maps
\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}
when
d^{\prime}>d
. Moreover, as the spaces of above equivariant decompositions are connected, the induced map is independent of the choices of the decompositions. Hence there is a well-defined morphism

{\mathfrak{Z}}(H,V,W)\to{\mathfrak{Z}}(G,X,Y).\qed

Similarly, from Proposition
3.15
one can define a morphism for embeddings of NC triples.

Lemma 3.22
.

Suppose
(H,V,W)\to(G,X,Y)
be an embedding of NC triples. Then there is a canonical morphism

(\mu_{*})^{-1}:{\mathfrak{Z}}(H,V,W)\to{\mathfrak{Z}}(G,X,Y).

Then the association
(G,V,W)\mapsto{\mathfrak{Z}}(G,V,W)
is a functor from the category
\underline{\smash{\mathbb{\Gamma}}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
to the category
\underline{\smash{\bf Str}}_{k}^{\rm NC}
. Then we define

{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\rm univ}:=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\operator@font colim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{(G,V,W)}{\mathfrak{Z}}(G,V,W)

which we call the set of
universal strata
(of virtual dimension
k
).

3.4.
Proof of Proposition
3.14

First notice that Proposition
3.14
reduces to the case that
V_{G}=W_{G}=0
. Hence within this subsection,
V
are
W
are complex representations of
G
containing no trivial subrepresentations. As a consequence

\widehat{\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W)={\rm Poly}{}_{G}^{d}(V,W).

Our proof closely resembles that of
[
Par13
, Lemma 4.11]
.

First we prove an algebraic result also used in
[
Par13
]
without providing a proof or reference.

Lemma 3.23
.

Suppose
V
and
W
are finite-dimensional complex representations of
G
. Then
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,W)
is a finitely generated module over the ring
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,{\mathbb{C}})
of
G
-invariant polynomials on
V
.

Proof.

The proof follows from
[
hk
]
, we present it here for completeness. By Hilbert’s basis theorem, given any finite-dimensional complex
G
-representation
V^{\prime}
, the ring
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})
is finitely generated. Now let
V^{\prime}=V\oplus W^{\vee}
where
W^{\vee}
is the dual to
W
endowed with the corresponding
G
-action. The ring
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})
has a bi-grading by keeping track of the degree of the
V
-coordinates and
W^{\vee}
-coordinates respectively. Choose
h_{1},\dots,h_{r},h_{r+1},\dots,h_{r+m},\dots,h_{n}
which generate
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})
such that
h_{1},\dots,h_{r}
have
W^{\vee}
-degree
0
,
h_{r+1},\dots,h_{r+m}
have
W^{\vee}
-degree
1
, and
h_{r+m+1},\dots,h_{n}
have higher
W^{\vee}
-degrees.

Note that
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,W)
can be identified with the subset of
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})
consisting of elements with
W^{\vee}
-degree
1
. Then any element in
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,W)
can be written as a linear combination of products of
h_{1},\dots,h_{r},h_{r+1},\dots,h_{r+m}
. Observe that
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
are indeed elements of
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,{\mathbb{C}})
, it shows
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,W)
is generated by
h_{r+1},\dots,h_{r+m}
as a module over
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

Next we construct a left inverse of the map
\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}
of (
3.1
).

Lemma 3.24
.

(cf.
[
Par13
, Lemma 4.11]
)
Given an NC triple
(G,V,W)
, there exists
d_{1}
such that for all
d^{\prime}>d\geq d_{1}
, there is a
G
-invariant holomorphic map

\rho_{d^{\prime}d}:M^{d^{\prime}}(G,V,W)\to M^{d}(G,V,W)

satisfying the following properties.

(1)

\rho_{d^{\prime}d}
is a submersion with connected fibers and preserves the action stratification.

(2)

\rho_{d^{\prime}d}\circ\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}
is the identity map of
M^{d}(G,V,W)
.

(3)

{\rm ev}\circ\rho_{d^{\prime}d}={\rm ev}:M^{d^{\prime}}(G,V,W)\to W
.

Proof of Proposition
3.14
.

Lemma
3.24
–(3) implies that
Z^{d^{\prime}}=\rho_{d^{\prime}d}^{-1}(Z^{d})
. By Lemma
3.24
–(1) and Proposition
B.20
,
\rho_{d^{\prime}d}^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, because
\rho_{d^{\prime}d}\circ\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}={\rm Id}
is transverse
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}
,
\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}
is transverse to
\rho_{d^{\prime}d}^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}
(see Lemma
B.4
). Hence

\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}=\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}^{*}\rho_{d^{\prime}d}^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}=(\rho_{d^{\prime}d}\circ\sigma_{d^{\prime}d})^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}={\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}.

Moreover, because
\rho_{d^{\prime}d}
is surjectve and has connected fibers, for each stratum
Z^{d}_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d}
,
\rho_{d^{\prime}d}^{-1}(Z^{d}_{\alpha})
is a single stratum of
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{d^{\prime}}
and
\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}
maps
Z_{\alpha}^{d}
into this stratum. Hence
\sigma
induces a bijection between the sets of strata.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.24
.

As
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,W)
is finitely generated over
{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,{\mathbb{C}})
(see Lemma
3.23
), one can find a sufficiently large
d_{0}
such that
{\rm Poly}_{G}^{d_{0}}(V,W)
contains a set of generators
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{m}
. Then when
d\geq d_{0}
, let
{\rm Homo}_{G}^{d}(V,W)\subseteq{\rm Poly}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
be the subset of
G
-equivariant homogeneous polynomial maps of degree
d
. Then

{\rm Poly}_{G}^{d^{\prime}}(V,W)={\rm Poly}_{G}^{d}(V,W)\oplus\bigoplus_{l=d+1}^{d^{\prime}}{\rm Homo}_{G}^{l}(V,W).

Choose a basis
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
of
\bigoplus_{l=d+1}^{d^{\prime}}{\rm Homo}_{G}^{l}(V,W)
, viewed as a
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector space. Then there exist polynomials
h_{ij}\in{\rm Poly}_{G}(V,{\mathbb{C}})
for
1\leq i\leq k
,
1\leq j\leq m
such that

P_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}h_{ij}Q_{j}.

Then for each
P\in{\rm Poly}_{G}^{d^{\prime}}(V,W)
, write
P=P^{\prime}+P^{\prime\prime}
with
P^{\prime}\in{\rm Poly}_{G}^{d}(V,W)
and
P^{\prime\prime}\in\bigoplus_{l=d+1}^{d^{\prime}}{\rm Homo}_{G}^{l}(V,W)
. We can write

P^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}P_{i}.

Then define

\rho_{d^{\prime}d}(v,P)=\left(v,P^{\prime}+\sum a_{i}h_{ij}(v)Q_{j}\right).

(3.7)

Abbreviate
\rho_{d^{\prime}d}
by
\rho
and
\sigma_{d^{\prime}d}
by
\sigma
. Then
\rho
is a submersion, preserves the action stratification, and satisfies
\rho\circ\sigma={\rm Id}_{M^{d}}
. Moreover, for all
(v,P)\in M^{d^{\prime}}
one has

{\rm ev}(\rho(v,P))=P^{\prime}(v)+\sum a_{i}h_{ij}(v)Q_{j}(v)=P^{\prime}(v)+P^{\prime\prime}(v)=P(v).

Lastly, we prove that
\rho
has connected fibers. For any
(v,P)\in M^{d^{\prime}}
with
P=P^{\prime}+P^{\prime\prime}
decomposed as above, consider the path
