to be the subset of smooth Whitney stratifications on
Z
whose restriction to
Z^{\prime}
coincides with
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
; we say that such Whitney stratifications
extend

{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
. Then for each open subset
U\subseteq M
, the restriction of Whitney stratifications induces a map

\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z;{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z\cap U;{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}|_{Z^{\prime}\cap U}).

Theorem B.13
.

There is a minimal element of
{\mathfrak{Z}}\in\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z;{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime})
unique up to equivalence such that for any open subset
U\subseteq M
, the restriction
{\mathfrak{Z}}|_{Z\cap U}
is a minimal element of
\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z\cap U;{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}|_{Z^{\prime}\cap U})
. In addition,
{\mathfrak{Z}}
is
strongly analytic.

Proof.

We follow the same strategy of the proof of Theorem
B.12
. As
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
is strongly complex analytic, for each
k
,
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k-1}^{\prime}
is a strongly complex analytic submanifold. Suppose
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(M)=n
. For each
k\leq n
, we would like to define a decreasing sequence of closed analytic subsets

Z\setminus Z^{\prime}={\mathfrak{Z}}_{n}^{\prime\prime}\supseteq{\mathfrak{Z}}_{n-1}^{\prime\prime}\cdots\supseteq{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}

such that for all
l\geq k
the following conditions are satisfied.

(1)

{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}
is a closed analytic set of dimension at most
l
.

(2)

{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l-1}^{\prime\prime}
is a strongly complex submanifold of real dimension
l
.

(3)

{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l-1}^{\prime\prime}
is disjoint from the closure of
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime}
.

(4)

For each
x\in{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l-1}^{\prime\prime}
and
m>l
, the pair
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{m-1}^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l-1}^{\prime\prime})
and the pair
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{m-1}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l-1}^{\prime\prime})
satisfy Whitney’s condition (b) at
x
.

Now we start with
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{n}^{\prime\prime}:=Z\setminus Z^{\prime}
which is indeed a closed analytic subset of
M
. Suppose we have constructed
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{n}^{\prime\prime},\ldots,{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
satisfying the above conditions. Now consider the closure of points
x\in{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
satisfying one of the following conditions, denoted by
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k-1}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
.

(1)

x
is contained in the closure of
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime}
.

(2)

x
is either a singular point of
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
or a regular point with
{\rm dim}_{x}({\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime})<k
.

(3)

x\in{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k,{\rm reg}}^{\prime\prime}
and there exists
m>k
such that the pair
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{m-1}^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k,{\rm reg}}^{\prime\prime})
does not satisfy Whitney’s condition (b) at
x
.

(4)

x\in{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k,{\rm reg}}^{\prime\prime}
and there exists
m>k
such that the pair
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{m-1}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k,{\rm reg}}^{\prime\prime})
does not satisfy Whitney’s condition (b) at
x
.

Then following the same argument as the proof of Theorem
B.12
,
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k-1}^{\prime\prime}
is a closed analytic set of real dimension at most
k-1
and hence
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k-1}^{\prime\prime}
is a strongly analytic submanifold. This finishes the inductive step. Then similar to Theorem
B.12
, one can find a Whitney stratification (of
Z\setminus Z^{\prime}
) by taking all connected components of
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k-1}^{\prime\prime}
. Together with strata of
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
, one obtains an extension of
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
, denoted by
{\mathfrak{Z}}
.

We need to prove that
{\mathfrak{Z}}
satisfies the minimality condition. Let
U\subseteq M
be an open subset. Let
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
be another Whitney stratification on
Z\cap U
which extends
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}|_{Z^{\prime}\cap U}
. Then the dimension filtrations of
{\mathfrak{Z}}|_{Z\cap U}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
are

\displaystyle\ {\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}\cap U=({\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime}\cap U)\sqcup({\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cap U),\

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}=({\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime}\cap U)\sqcup\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}

where
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq(Z\setminus Z^{\prime})\cap U
is the dimension filtration of the restriction of
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
to
(Z\setminus S^{\prime})\cap U
. Then we need to show that either
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cap U=\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
for all
l
or there exists
k
such that it is true for all
l>k
and
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cap U\subsetneq\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
. We argue inductively. For
n={\rm dim}(M)
, one has
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{n}^{\prime\prime}\cap U=\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{n}^{\prime\prime}=(Z\setminus Z^{\prime})\cap U
by definition. Suppose we have proved that
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}\cap U=\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{l}^{\prime\prime}
for all
l>k
. Assume in contradiction that
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cap U\nsubseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
. Then there exists a point
x\in{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cap U
which is not contained in
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
, namely,
x\in\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
. As
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
is closed,
x
is a smooth (equivalently analytic) regular point of
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1}^{\prime\prime}={\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1}^{\prime\prime}\cap U
of local (real) dimension
k+1
.

Now because
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a Whitney stratification on
Z\cap U
and
x\in\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
, it follows from Lemma
B.1
,
x\notin\overline{{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1}^{\prime}}\cap U
. Then as
x\in{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cap U\subseteq{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
, by the construction of
{\mathfrak{Z}}
, it follows that there is a sequence
x_{i}\to x
and
m>k
such that either
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m+1}^{\prime\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{m}^{\prime\prime},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1,{\rm reg}}^{\prime\prime})
or
({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m+1}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathfrak{Z}}_{m}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1,{\rm reg}}^{\prime\prime})
does not satisfy Whitney’s condition (b) at
x_{i}
. This contradicts the hypothesis that
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a Whitney stratification on
Z\cap U
. Therefore
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\cap U\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
, hence inductively we conclude that
{\mathfrak{Z}}|_{U\cap Z}\leq\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
.
∎

B.4.
Proof of Proposition
3.6

We first construct the Whitney stratification. Choose an increasing order of
\{M_{\alpha}\}
as
M_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,M_{\alpha_{m}}
such that
M_{\alpha_{i}}\leq M_{\alpha_{j}}
implies
i\leq j
. For all
k
, denote

M^{(k)}:=M\setminus\bigcup_{i<k}M_{\alpha_{i}}

which gives a filtration

M=M^{(1)}\supset M^{(2)}\supset\cdots\supset M^{(m)}.

Denote

Z^{(k)}:=Z\cap M^{(k)}

which is a stronlgy complex analytic subset of
M
. Then for each
k
,
\overline{Z^{(k)}}
is analytic.

As the first step of the induction, by Theorem
B.12
, there exists a canonical Whitney stratification of the closure
\overline{Z^{(m)}}
whose strata are strongly complex analytic submanifolds of
M
. Restrict to the open subset
Z^{(m)}
to obtain a Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(m)}
which is still strongly complex analytic.

We assume, as the induction hypothesis, that for a
k\leq m
, we have constructed strongly complex analytic Whitney stratifications
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(k)}
on
Z^{(k)}
such that for each
i\geq k
,
Z_{\alpha_{i}}
is the union of strata. Now consider the closure
\overline{Z^{(k-1)}}
which contains the open subset
Z^{(k)}
such that
\overline{Z^{(k-1)}}\setminus Z^{(k)}
is closed analytic. Then by Theorem
B.13
, there exists a minimal extension of
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(k)}
to
\overline{Z^{(k-1)}}
. Restrict the extension to
Z^{(k-1)}
(which contains
Z^{(k)}
), one obtains a stronlgy complex analytic Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(k-1)}
. Then the induction can continue until we find a nice strongly complex analytic Whitney stratification on
Z
.

Lastly, one can combine induction with the proof of the minimality part of Theorem
B.13
to show that the constructed Whitney stratification is minimal among all smooth Whitney stratifications of
Z
which respect
{\mathfrak{M}}
. ∎

We have the following invariance property of the canonical Whitney stratification which respects the given stratification.

Proposition B.14
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold equipped with a partition
{\mathfrak{M}}
. Let
Z\subseteq M
be a closed subset and let
{\mathfrak{Z}}\in\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z,{\mathfrak{M}})
be a minimal element. Let
f
be a diffeomorphism of
M
which preserves
{\mathfrak{M}}
such that
f(Z)=Z
. Then
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}\equiv{\mathfrak{Z}}
. If
{\mathfrak{Z}}
has connected strata, then
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}={\mathfrak{Z}}
.

Proof.

The assumption implies that
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}\in\mathcal{WS}^{\infty}(Z;{\mathfrak{M}})
. Then by Corollary
B.7
, one has
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}\equiv{\mathfrak{Z}}
. Suppose
{\mathfrak{Z}}
has connected strata. As
f
is a diffeomorphism, for each stratum
Z_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}
which is connected,
f^{-1}(Z_{\alpha})
, a stratum of
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}
, is connected. Hence
f^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}={\mathfrak{Z}}
.
∎

B.5.
Proof of Proposition
3.8

In this subsection we prove Proposition
3.8
. First we gather some basic facts about product Whitney stratifications. Let
M
and
N
be smooth manifolds, and
S\subseteq M
and
T\subseteq N
be subsets equipped with a Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{S}}
resp.
{\mathfrak{T}}
. Denote

\displaystyle\ R=S\times T,\ \tilde{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{S}}\times{\mathfrak{T}}.

Then the dimension filtration of
\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}
is

\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}=\bigcup_{k+l=m}{\mathfrak{S}}_{k}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}

while

\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}^{*}=\bigsqcup_{k+l=m}{\mathfrak{S}}_{k}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}^{*}

which is the disjoint union.

We first consider the absolute case of Proposition
3.8
.

Proposition B.15
.

Let
M
resp.
N
be a complex manifold and
S\subseteq M
resp.
T\subseteq N
be a closed analytic set equipped with the canonical Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{S}}
resp.
{\mathfrak{T}}
(provided by Proposition
3.6
). Then the canonical Whitney stratification of
R:=S\times T\subseteq M\times M
is equal to
{\mathfrak{S}}\times{\mathfrak{T}}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
be the canonical Whitney stratification on
R
. Then
{\mathfrak{R}}\leq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}
. As both stratifications have connected strata, we only need to prove that they are equivalent, i.e., having the same dimension filtration. We prove by contradiction. Suppose
{\mathfrak{R}}
is not equivalent to
\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}
. Then by definition, there exists an
m
such that

{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{n}\ \forall n\geq m\ {\rm and}\ {\mathfrak{R}}_{m-1}\subsetneq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m-1}.

Therefore, there exists a point

r=(s,t)\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}=\bigcup_{k+l=m}{\mathfrak{S}}_{k}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{l},

and
r\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m-1}
,
r\notin{\mathfrak{R}}_{m-1}
. Then
r=(s,t)\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}^{*}\subseteq{\mathfrak{R}}_{m,{\rm reg}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m,{\rm reg}}
. Hence there exist
k,l
with
k+l=m
such that
s\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{k,{\rm reg}}
and
t\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{l,{\rm reg}}
. On the other hand, the condition that
r=(s,t)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m-1}
implies that either
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k-1}
or
t\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{l-1}
. Indeed, if
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k}^{*}
and
t\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}^{*}
, then
r\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}^{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}^{*}
, contradicting that
r\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{m-1}
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k-1}
. Then there exist a sequence
s_{\mu}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k^{\prime}}^{*}
, a sequence
s_{\mu}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k,{\rm reg}}
, both converging to
s
, such that
T_{s_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{k^{\prime}}^{*}
converges to a subspace
H_{s}\subseteq T_{s}M
, the secant line
\overline{s_{\mu}s_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converges to a line
L_{s}\subseteq T_{s}M
, and
L_{s}\nsubseteq H_{s}
. Now depending on the position of
t
we discuss in two cases.

(1)

t\notin{\mathfrak{T}}_{l-1}
. Then consider the sequence of points
r_{\mu}=(s_{\mu},t)\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l}
which converges to
r=(s,t)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Then for
\mu
sufficiently large,
r_{\mu}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Consider the sequence of points
r_{\mu}^{\prime}=(s_{\mu}^{\prime},t)\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k^{\prime}}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}^{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k^{\prime}+l}^{*}
which also converges to
r
. Then the sequence of tangent planes
T_{r_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{k^{\prime}+l}^{*}=T_{s_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{k^{\prime}}^{*}\oplus T_{t}{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}^{*}
converges to
H_{s}\oplus T_{t}{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}^{*}
; the sequence of secant lines
\overline{r_{\mu}r_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converges to the line
L_{s}\oplus\{0\}
which is not contained in
H_{s}\oplus T_{t}{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}^{*}
. Hence condition (b) for
(\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k^{\prime}+l}^{*},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l,{\rm reg}})
fails at
r
.

(2)

t\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{l-1}
. Then it follows that for some
l^{\prime}>l
, condition (b) for
({\mathfrak{T}}_{l^{\prime}}^{*},{\mathfrak{T}}_{l,{\rm reg}})
fails at
t
. Hence there exist a sequence
t_{\mu}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}
and a sequence
t_{\mu}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{l,{\rm reg}}
both converging to
t
such that
T_{t_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{T}}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}
converges to a subspace
H_{t}\subseteq T_{t}N
, the secant lines
\overline{t_{\mu}t_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converge to a line
L_{t}\subseteq T_{t}N
, and
L_{t}\nsubseteq H_{t}
. Then consider the sequence
r_{\mu}=(s_{\mu},t_{\mu})
which converges to
r\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Then for
\mu
sufficiently large,
r_{\mu}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Consider the sequence
r_{\mu}^{\prime}=(s_{\mu}^{\prime},t_{\mu}^{\prime})\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{k^{\prime}}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}^{*}
which also converges to
r
. The tangent planes
T_{r_{\mu}^{\prime}}\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}^{*}
converge to
H_{s}\oplus H_{t}
while the secant lines
\overline{r_{\mu}r_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converge to a line
L_{r}\subseteq L_{s}\oplus L_{t}
. Then
L_{r}\nsubseteq H_{s}\oplus H_{t}
. Hence condition (b) for
(\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}^{*},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l,{\rm reg}})
fails at
r
. ∎

In both cases, by the induction hypothesis, it follows that
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{k+l-1}={\mathfrak{R}}_{m-1}
, contradicting the assumption that
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}^{*}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

Abbreviate
S_{\alpha}=S\cap M_{\alpha}
,
T_{\beta}=T\cap N_{\beta}
with corresponding Whitney stratifications
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha}
and
{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta}
. Abbreviate
R=S\times T
and
R_{\alpha\beta}=S_{\alpha}\times T_{\beta}
. Let the canonical Whintey stratification on
R
resp.
R_{\alpha\beta}
be
{\mathfrak{R}}
resp.
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta}
and the product Whitney stratification be
\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}
resp.
\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta}
.

We prove this proposition by induction on
(\alpha,\beta)
. First, Proposition
B.15
implies that for any top stratum
R_{\alpha\beta}\subseteq M\times N
,
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta}
. Now fix a pair
(\alpha,\beta)
, suppose we have shown that for any
R_{\gamma\delta}>R_{\alpha\beta}
,
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\gamma\delta}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\gamma\delta}
. We would like to show that
{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta}
. Suppose our claim is false. Then there exists
m\geq 0
such that

{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,n}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,n}\ (\forall n\geq m)\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ {\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m-1}\subsetneq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m-1}.

Then one can choose a point
r=(s,t)
such that

\displaystyle\ r\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m}^{*},\

\displaystyle\ r\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m-1}.

Then

r=(s,t)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m-1}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m}=\bigcup_{p+q=m}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,p}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q}={\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m}.

(B.2)

Then
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m}^{*}\subseteq{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m,{\rm reg}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m,{\rm reg}}
. Therefore for some pair
(p,q)
with
p+q=m
,
(s,t)
is a regular point of
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,p}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q}
of local dimension
p+q
. Hence

\displaystyle\ s\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,p,{\rm reg}},\

\displaystyle\ t\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q,{\rm reg}}.

Similar to the proof of the absolute case, we know that either
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,p-1}
or
t\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q-1}
. Without loss of generality, assume that
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,p-1}
.

Claim.
For all
r>\alpha
,
p^{\prime}\leq p
,
\delta>\beta
,
q^{\prime}\leq q
,

\displaystyle\ s\notin\partial{\mathfrak{S}}_{\gamma,p^{\prime}}^{*},\

\displaystyle\ t\notin\partial{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q^{\prime}}^{*}.

Proof of the claim.
Suppose for some
\gamma>\alpha
and
p^{\prime}\leq p
,
s\in\partial{\mathfrak{S}}_{\gamma,p^{\prime}}^{*}
. Let
q^{\prime}\leq q
be such that
t\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q^{\prime}}^{*}
. Then we see that

r=(s,t)\in\partial{\mathfrak{S}}_{\gamma,p^{\prime}}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q^{\prime}}^{*}\subseteq\partial\left({\mathfrak{S}}_{\gamma,p^{\prime}}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,q^{\prime}}^{*}\right)\subseteq\partial\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\gamma\beta,p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}^{*}=\partial{\mathfrak{R}}_{\gamma\beta,p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}^{*}.

Here the last equality follows from the induction hypothesis. Notice that
p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}\leq m
and
R_{\gamma\beta}>R_{\alpha\beta}
. This contradicts the fact that
r\notin{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m-1}
and the way to construct the canonical nice Whitney stratification
{\mathfrak{R}}
. The claimed condition for
t
can be proved in the same way.
End of the proof of the claim.

The rest of the argument is similar to the last part of the proof of Proposition
B.15
, except that we need to use the above claim. The assumption
s\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,p-1}
and the above claim imply that there exists
k^{\prime}>p
such that condition (b) for
({\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k^{\prime}}^{*},{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k,{\rm reg}})
fails at
s
. Then there exist a sequence
s_{\mu}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k^{\prime}}^{*}
, a sequence
s_{\mu}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k,{\rm reg}}
, both converging to
s
, such that
T_{s_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k^{\prime}}^{*}
converges to a subspace
H_{s}\subseteq T_{s}M_{\alpha}
, the secant line
\overline{s_{\mu}s_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converges to a line
L_{s}\subseteq T_{s}M_{\alpha}
, and
L_{s}\nsubseteq H_{s}
. Now depending on the position of
t
we discuss in two cases.

(1)

Assume
t\notin{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l-1}
. Then consider the sequence of points
r_{\mu}=(s_{\mu},t)\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k+l}
which converges to
r=(s,t)\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Then for
\mu
sufficiently large,
r_{\mu}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Consider the sequence of points
r_{\mu}^{\prime}=(s_{\mu}^{\prime},t)\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k^{\prime}}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l}^{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k^{\prime}+l}^{*}
which also converges to
r
. Then the sequence of tangent planes
T_{r_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k^{\prime}+l}^{*}=T_{s_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k^{\prime}}^{*}\oplus T_{t}{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}^{*}
converges to
H_{s}\oplus T_{t}{\mathfrak{S}}_{l}^{*}
; the sequence of secant lines
\overline{r_{\mu}r_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converges to the line
L_{s}\oplus\{0\}
which is not contained in
H_{s}\oplus T_{t}{\mathfrak{T}}_{l}^{*}
. Hence condition (b) for
(\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k^{\prime}+l}^{*},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k+l,{\rm reg}})
fails at
r
.

(2)

t\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l-1}
. Then the above claim implies that for some
l^{\prime}>l
, condition (b) for
({\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l^{\prime}}^{*},{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l,{\rm reg}})
fails at
t
. Hence there exist a sequence
t_{\mu}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l^{\prime}}^{*}
and a sequence
t_{\mu}\in{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l,{\rm reg}}
both converging to
t
such that
T_{t_{\mu}^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l^{\prime}}^{*}
converges to a subspace
H_{t}\subseteq T_{t}N_{\beta}
, the secant lines
\overline{t_{\mu}t_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converge to a line
L_{t}\subseteq T_{t}N_{\beta}
, and
L_{t}\nsubseteq H_{t}
. Then consider the sequence
r_{\mu}=(s_{\mu},t_{\mu})
which converges to
r\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Then for
\mu
sufficiently large,
r_{\mu}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k+l,{\rm reg}}
. Consider the sequence
r_{\mu}^{\prime}=(s_{\mu}^{\prime},t_{\mu}^{\prime})\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\alpha,k^{\prime}}^{*}\times{\mathfrak{T}}_{\beta,l^{\prime}}^{*}\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}^{*}
which also converges to
r
. The tangent planes
T_{r_{\mu}^{\prime}}\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}^{*}
converge to
H_{s}\oplus H_{t}
while the secant lines
\overline{r_{\mu}r_{\mu}^{\prime}}
converge to a line
L_{r}\subseteq L_{s}\oplus L_{t}
. Then
L_{r}\nsubseteq H_{s}\oplus H_{t}
. Hence condition (b) for
(\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}}^{*},\tilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,k+l,{\rm reg}})
fails at
r
.

In both cases, by the induction hypothesis it follows that
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m-1}
which contradicts the assumption that
r\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha\beta,m}^{*}
.
∎

B.6.
Pullback under submersions

This subsection serves as a technical preparation for proving several statements in Section
3
.

First notice that Whitney’s condition (b) is invariant under diffeomorphisms. In fact it is preserved under pullbacks by submersions.

Lemma B.16
.

Let
X,Y
be disjoint smooth submanifolds of
M
. Let
\pi:\tilde{M}\to M
be a submersion. Denote
\tilde{X}=\pi^{-1}(X)
,
\tilde{Y}=\pi^{-1}(Y)
. Then
(X,Y)
satisfies Whitney’s condition (b) at
y\in\pi(\tilde{Y})\subseteq Y
if and only if
(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})
satisfies Whitney’s condition (b) at
\tilde{y}\in\tilde{Y}
for all
\tilde{y}\in\pi^{-1}(y)
.

Proof.

As
\pi
is a submersion, it is transverse to both
X
and
Y
. Then the Whitney condition is preserved under transversal pullbacks (see for example
[
GWdPL76
, (1.3)]
). Conversely, suppose
(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})
satifies condition (b) at all points
\tilde{y}\in\pi^{-1}(y)
. Suppose
x_{i}\in X
converges to
y
,
y_{i}\in Y
converges to
y
,
\overline{x_{i}y_{i}}
converges to a line
l\subseteq T_{y}M
, and
T_{x_{i}}X
converges to
\tau\subseteq T_{y}M
. Choose a preimage
\tilde{y}\in\pi^{-1}(y)
. Then there exist nearby points
\tilde{x}_{i}\in\pi^{-1}(x_{i})\subseteq\tilde{X}
converging to
\tilde{y}
and
\tilde{y}_{i}\in\pi^{-1}(y_{i})\subseteq\tilde{Y}
converging to
\tilde{y}
. By choosing a subsequence, we may assume
\overline{\tilde{x}_{i}\tilde{y}_{i}}
converges to
\tilde{l}\subseteq T_{\tilde{y}}\tilde{M}
and
T_{\tilde{x}_{i}}\tilde{X}
converges to
\tilde{\tau}\subseteq T_{\tilde{y}}\tilde{M}
. We may also move
\tilde{y}_{i}
in the same fiber of
\pi
to guarantee that
\tilde{l}\nsubseteq{\rm ker}(d\pi)
. Then
d\pi(\tilde{l})=l
and

d\pi(\tilde{\tau})=d\pi\left(\lim_{i\to\infty}T_{\tilde{x}_{i}}\tilde{U}\right)=\lim_{i\to\infty}d\pi(T_{\tilde{x}_{i}}\tilde{U})=\lim_{i\to\infty}T_{x_{i}}U=\tau.

Then
\tilde{l}\subseteq\tilde{\tau}
which implies
l\subseteq\tau
. Hence
(X,Y)
satisfies condition (b) at
y
.
∎

One has the following straightforward corollary.

Corollary B.17
.

When
\pi
is a surjective submersion,
S_{b}(\tilde{X},\tilde{Y})=\pi^{-1}(S_{b}(X,Y))
.

There are some other easy results about surjective submersions in complex analytic category.

Lemma B.18
.

Let
M
be a complex manifold with
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(M)=m
and
U\subseteq M
. Let
\pi:\tilde{M}\to M
be a surjective analytic submersion and
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\tilde{M})=\tilde{m}
. Then

(1)

\pi^{-1}(\overline{U})=\overline{\pi^{-1}(U)}
.

(2)

If
U
is a closed analytic subset, then

\pi^{-1}(U)_{\rm sing}=\pi^{-1}(U_{\rm sing})

and for each
l
,

\pi^{-1}(U)_{\tilde{m}-l,{\rm reg}}=\pi^{-1}(U_{m-l,{\rm reg}}).

(3)

If
U\subseteq M
is strongly analytic, then
\pi^{-1}(U)\subseteq\tilde{M}
is also strongly analytic.

Proof.

For item (1), the inclusion
\overline{\pi^{-1}(U)}\subseteq\pi^{-1}(\overline{U})
follows from definition; for the reversed inclusion, it can be proven using the surjective property of
\pi
and choosing a product neighborhood of limiting points in
\overline{U}
. For item (2), note that
\tilde{p}\in\pi^{-1}(U)
is a smooth point if and only if
p=\pi(\tilde{p})\in U
is a smooth point, so it follows by taking the complement. For item (3), by assumption, both
\overline{U}
and
\overline{U}\setminus U
are closed and analytic; it follows from (1) that both
\overline{\pi^{-1}(U)}=\pi^{-1}(\overline{U})
and
\overline{\pi^{-1}(U)}\setminus\pi^{-1}(U)
are closed and analytic subsets, so (3) is proved.
∎

B.6.1.
Pulling back minimal Whitney stratifications

Let
M
be a complex manifold and
\pi:\tilde{M}\to M
be a surjective analytic submersion. Suppose
Z\subseteq M
is a strongly analytic subset and
{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a strongly analytic Whitney stratification of
Z
, then by Lemma
B.18
, the pullback Whitney stratification
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}
is also strongly analytic.

Corollary B.19
.

Let
M
be a complex manifold,
Z\subseteq M
be a closed analytic subset,
Z^{\prime}\subseteq Z
be an open subset such that
Z\setminus Z^{\prime}
is a closed analytic subset. Suppose
\pi:\tilde{M}\to M
is a complex analytic submersion with
Z\subseteq\pi(\tilde{M})
. Let
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
be a strongly analytic Whitney stratification of
Z^{\prime}
and let
{\mathfrak{Z}}
be a minimal extension to
Z
. Then
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a minimal extension to
\pi^{-1}(Z)
of the pullback
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
on
\pi^{-1}(Z^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The proof is based on tracing the steps in the proof of Theorem
B.13
. Suppose

{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(M)=m
and
{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\tilde{M})=\tilde{m}
. Following the recipe of the proof of Theorem
B.13
, one can construct a minimal extension
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
of
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}
by constructing a decreasing sequence

\pi^{-1}(Z\setminus Z^{\prime})=\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\tilde{m}}^{\prime\prime}\supseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\tilde{m}-1}^{\prime\prime}\cdots\supseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}.

We claim that for
l=0,1,\ldots
,

\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\tilde{m}-l}^{\prime\prime}\equiv\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m-l}^{\prime\prime}).

This would imply that
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}\equiv\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}
. Indeed, the set
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}^{\prime\prime}
is defined as the closure of points in
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k-1}^{\prime\prime}
which satisfy one of the conditions listed in the proof of Theorem
B.13
. Corollary
B.17
and Lemma
B.18
imply that these conditions are preserved under the pullback by the submersion
\pi
. Therefore,
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}_{\tilde{m}-l}^{\prime\prime}\equiv\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{Z}}_{m-l}^{\prime\prime})
.
∎

There is another technical situation we need to consider.

Proposition B.20
.

Let
M,{\mathfrak{M}},Z
be as in Proposition
3.6
. Let
{\mathfrak{Z}}
be a minimal Whitney stratification of
Z
which respects
{\mathfrak{M}}
. Let
\tilde{M}
be a complex manifold and
\pi:\tilde{M}\to M
be a surjective analytic submersion. Notice that there is the pullback stratification

\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}=\{\tilde{M}_{\alpha}\}=\{\pi^{-1}(M_{\alpha})\}

of
\tilde{M}
whose strata are strongly analytic submanifolds. Then the pullback Whitney stratification
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a minimal one on
\pi^{-1}(Z)
which respests
\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}
.

Proof.

We go through the construction in the proof of Proposition
3.6
for both
Z
and
\tilde{Z}:=\pi^{-1}(Z)
in parallel. Order the strata
M_{\alpha}
increasingly as

M_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,M_{\alpha_{m}}

such that
M_{\alpha_{i}}\leq M_{\alpha_{j}}
implies
i\leq j
. Define

\displaystyle\ M^{(k)}:=M\setminus\bigcup_{i<k}M_{\alpha_{i}},\

\displaystyle\ \tilde{M}^{(k)}:=\tilde{M}\setminus\bigcup_{i<k}\tilde{M}_{\alpha_{i}}=\pi^{-1}(M^{(k)}).

Set

\displaystyle\ Z^{(k)}:=Z\cap M^{(k)},\

\displaystyle\ \tilde{Z}^{(k)}:=\tilde{Z}\cap\tilde{M}^{(k)}=\pi^{-1}(Z^{(k)}).

Then following the proof of Proposition
3.6
for both
Z
and
\tilde{Z}
, we have the strongly analytic Whitney stratifications
{\mathfrak{Z}}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
respecting
{\mathfrak{M}}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}
respectively. For each
k
, let
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(k)}
resp.
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(k)}
be the restriction of
{\mathfrak{Z}}
resp.
\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}
on the open subset
Z^{(k)}
resp.
\tilde{Z}^{(k)}
. We prove by a reversed induction on
k
that for each
k
, one has

\tilde{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(k)}=\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(k)}.

(B.3)

When
k=m
, recall that
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(m)}
is the restriction of the canonical Whitney stratification on
\overline{Z^{(m)}}
to
Z^{(m)}
. Then the absolute case (i.e. the
Z^{\prime}=\emptyset
case) of Corollary
B.19
shows that the canonical Whitney stratification on
\overline{\tilde{Z}^{(m)}}=\pi^{-1}(\overline{Z^{(m)}})
is equivalent to the pullback of the canonical Whitney stratification on
\overline{Z^{(m)}}
. Then the restriction to
\tilde{Z}^{(m)}
is equivalent to
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{Z}}^{(m)}
. The relative case of Corollary
B.19
allows us to prove (
B.3
) inductively.
∎

Appendix C
Thom–Mather Stratified Spaces

In this appendix we review the notion of Thom–Mather stratified spaces (or called abstract stratified spaces), especially their fundamental classes. It was proved by Mather
[
Mat12
]
that Whitney stratified sets in smooth manifolds are Thom–Mather stratified spaces. The zero locus of an FOP transverse section is a stratified space inside an effective orbifold which satisfies similar properties as a Whitney stratified set in a manifold. In order to construct a fundamental class, we extend Mather’s result to this kind of stratified subsets in orbifolds.

C.1.
Thom–Mather stratified spaces

Thom–Mather stratified spaces are spaces stratified by smooth manifolds with certain structures regularizing the normal behavior near each stratum. Since the definitions in standard references (
[
Tho64
,
Tho69
]
[
Mat12
]
) require the control data as part of the structure, which is not canonically given in relevant constructions, we modify the definition in the following way.

Definition C.1
.

A
Thom–Mather stratified space
is a stratified space
(X,{\mathfrak{X}})
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

X
is a locally compact, Hausdorff, and second countable space.

(2)

{\mathfrak{X}}
is a stratification such that each stratum
X_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
is a smooth manifold.

(3)

There exists a set of
control data
, which is a collection

{\mathfrak{J}}=\Big\{(N_{\alpha},\pi_{\alpha},\rho_{\alpha})\ |\ X_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{X}}\Big\}

where
N_{\alpha}\subseteq X
is an open neighborhood of
X_{\alpha}
,
\pi_{\alpha}:N_{\alpha}\to X_{\alpha}
is a continuous retraction, and
\rho_{\alpha}:N_{\alpha}\to[0,+\infty)
is a continuous function. Moreover, these triples satisfy the following conditions.

(a)

X_{\alpha}=\{v\in N_{\alpha}\ |\ \rho_{\alpha}(v)=0\}
.

(b)

For each pair of strata
X_{\alpha},X_{\beta}\in{\mathfrak{X}}
, define
N_{\beta\alpha}=N_{\alpha}\cap X_{\beta}
(which is an open subset of the manifold
X_{\beta}
),
\pi_{\beta\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}|_{N_{\beta\alpha}}
, and
\rho_{\beta\alpha}=\rho_{\alpha}|_{N_{\beta\alpha}}
. We require that the map

(\pi_{\beta\alpha},\rho_{\beta\alpha}):N_{\beta\alpha}\to X_{\alpha}\times(0,+\infty)

is a smooth submersion.

(c)

For any three strata
X_{\alpha},X_{\beta},X_{\gamma}
one has

\displaystyle\ \pi_{\beta\alpha}\circ\pi_{\gamma\beta}=\pi_{\gamma\alpha},\

\displaystyle\ \rho_{\beta\alpha}\circ\pi_{\gamma\beta}=\rho_{\gamma\alpha}

whenever both sides of the equations are defined.

The
dimension
of a Thom–Mather stratified space
X
is the maximum of dimensions of its strata. The
dimension filtration
of
X
is the filtration

X_{n}\supseteq X_{n-1}\supseteq\cdots

where
X_{k}
is the union of strata of dimension at most
k
. Notice that the union of any subset of strata is naturally a Thom–Mather stratified space. On the other hand, any open subset of
X
is also a Thom–Mather stratified space.

Definition C.2
.

An
isomorphism
of Thom–Mather stratified spaces from
X
to
Y
is a homeomorphism
f:X\to Y
whose restriction to each stratum
X_{\alpha}\subseteq X
is a diffeomorphism onto a stratum of
Y
. An
open embedding
of Thom–Mather stratified space is an isomorphism of Thom–Mather stratified space onto an open subset of the codomain.

Goresky
[
Gor78
]
proved that Thom–Mather stratified spaces admits triangulations.

Theorem C.3
.

[
Gor78
, 5. Proposition]
Let
(X,{\mathfrak{X}})
be a Thom–Mather stratified space. Then there exists a polyhedral complex
K
and a homeomorphism
f:|K|\to X
such that for each stratum
X_{\alpha}
,
f^{-1}(\overline{X_{\alpha}})
is a subcomplex and
f:f^{-1}(X_{\alpha})\to X_{\alpha}
is a smooth triangulation.
10
10

10

Goresky’s construction
a priori
depends on the control data.

We would like to show that certain Thom–Mather stratified spaces admit fundamental classes. Let
X
be a compact
n
-dimensional Thom–Mather stratified space.

Lemma C.4
.

If
k>l
, then
H_{k}(X_{l};{\mathbb{Z}})=0
.

Proof.

Each
X_{l}
itself is a Thom–Mather stratified space. Then the lemma follows from Goresky’s theorem.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

[
Gor76
, 0.5.1 Proposition]
Given a control data on
(X,{\mathfrak{X}})
, for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, let
N_{\alpha}(\epsilon):=\rho_{\alpha}^{-1}([0,\epsilon))\subseteq N_{\alpha}\subseteq X
. Define

O_{n-1}(\epsilon):=\bigcup_{{\rm dim}(X_{\alpha})\leq n-1}N_{\alpha}(\epsilon).

Then for
\epsilon
sufficiently small, the inclusion map
X_{n-1}\to O_{n-1}(\epsilon)
is a homotopy equivalence.

Now suppose each top stratum of an
n
-dimensional Thom–Mather stratified space
X
is oriented. Abbreviate
O_{n-1}(\epsilon)
by
O_{n-1}
. Then there exists a fundamental class

[X]\in H_{n}(X,O_{n-1})

(in integer coefficients). The homotopy equivalence
X_{n-1}\simeq O_{n-1}
implies

H_{n}(X,X_{n-1})\cong H_{n}(X,O_{n-1}).

Hence we regard
[X]
as a class in
H_{n}(X,X_{n-1})
. We call it the
fundamental class
of
X
(with respect to the orientations on the top strata). If we are given a triangulation of
X
, then the fundamental class is represented by the singular cycle which is the sum of all the oriented top-dimensional cells of the triangulation.

Definition C.6
.

An
n
-dimensional
Thom–Mather stratified pseudomanifold
is an
n
-dimensional Thom–Mather stratified space
X
such that
X_{n-1}=X_{n-2}
.

If
X
is a compact oriented Thom–Mather stratified pseudomanifold, the exact sequence

H_{n}(X)\to H_{n}(X,X_{n-1})\to H_{n-1}(X_{n-1})=H_{n-1}(X_{n-2})=\{0\},

where the last equality follows from Lemma
C.4
, implies that
[X]
lives in
H_{n}(X;{\mathbb{Z}})
. We record this discussion as follows.

Proposition C.7
.

Any
n
-dimensional Thom–Mather stratified pseudomanifold
X
with oriented top-dimensional strata has a well-defined fundamental class

[X]\in H_{n}(X;{\mathbb{Z}}).

Definition C.8
.

A
cobordism
of compact oriented
n
-dimensional Thom–Mather stratified pseudomanifolds from
Y
to
Z
consists of a compact oriented
n+1
-dimensional Thom–Mather stratified space
X
, a closed union of strata
\partial X\subseteq X
, and an isomorphism
Y\sqcup Z\cong\partial X
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

X\setminus\partial X
is an
n+1
-dimensional Thom–Mather stratified pseudomanifold.

(2)

There exists an open embedding
\partial X\times[0,\epsilon)\to X
whose restriction to
\partial X\times\{0\}
is the identity map of
\partial X
. This implies that top strata of
\partial X
have naturally induced orientations.

(3)

The isomorphism
Y\sqcup Z\cong\partial X
is orientation preserving if
Y\sqcup Z
is oriented as
(-Y)\sqcup Z
.

In the situation of the above definition,
X_{n}=X_{n-1}\cup\partial X
. Consider the fundamental class
[X]\in H_{n+1}(X,X_{n})
and the exact sequence

H_{n+1}(X_{n},\partial X)\to H_{n+1}(X,\partial X)\to H_{n+1}(X,X_{n})\to H_{n}(X_{n},\partial X).

By the collar structure, excision, and the fact that
X_{n}\setminus\partial X=X_{n-1}\setminus\partial X
, it follows that
H_{n+1}(X_{n},\partial X)=H_{n}(X_{n},\partial X)=0
. Hence
[X]
lives in
H_{n+1}(X,\partial X)
.

Lemma C.9
.

The image of
[X]
under the boundary map
H_{n+1}(X,\partial X)\to H_{n}(\partial X)
is
[\partial X]=[Z]-[Y]
.

Proof.

By Theorem
C.3
, one can choose a triangulation of
X
. We see that the codimension one faces of the top-dimensional cells, if not cancelled in pairs, are exactly the top-dimensional faces of the boundary
\partial X
.
∎

C.2.
Tubular neighborhoods in orbifolds

C.2.1.
Tubular neighborhoods in manifolds

We recall several useful notions and results about tubular neighborhoods in smooth manifolds towards constructing control data on Whitney stratified subsets of orbifolds. The main reference is
[
Mat12
, Section 6]
. Let
(M,g)
be a smooth manifold and
S\subseteq M
be a closed submanifold. Recall the notion of normal bundle
NS\to S
and disk bundle
N^{\epsilon}S\subseteq NS
of radius
\sqrt{\epsilon}
(defined with respect to the inner product on
NS
induced from
g
) associated to a smooth function
\epsilon:S\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(cf. (
2.2
)). We need to consider identifications of disk bundles in
NS
and tubular neighborhoods beyond those obtained by normal exponential maps.

Definition C.10
.

Let
(M,g)
and
S
be as above.

(1)

A
tubular neighborhood
of
S
is a pair
T_{S}=(\epsilon,\varphi)
where
\epsilon:S\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a smooth function and
\varphi:N^{\epsilon}S\to M
is an open embedding satisfying the following condition. For any
x\in S
,
\varphi(x)=x
and composed linear map

\textstyle{N_{x}S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\varphi_{x}}
\textstyle{T_{x}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{x}S}

is the identity map of
N_{x}S
. In particular,
d\varphi|_{S}
gives a splitting of the canonical exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{TS\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.44095pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 73.44095pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

(2)

Associated to each tubular neighborhood
T_{S}
, denote

|T_{S}|=\varphi(N^{\epsilon}S),

over which there is a
tubular projection

\pi_{S}:=\pi_{NS}\circ\varphi^{-1}:|T_{S}|\to S

and the
tubular function

\rho_{S}:|T_{S}|\to[0,+\infty),\ \rho_{S}(y)=\|\varphi^{-1}(y)\|^{2}.

(3)

The
restriction
of a tubular neighborhood
T_{S}
of
S
to an open subset
U\subseteq S
is the tubular neighborhood
T_{S}|_{U}:=(\epsilon|_{U},\varphi|_{N^{\epsilon}S|_{U}})
of
U
.

(4)

Two tubular neighborhoods
T_{S}=(\epsilon,\varphi)
and
T_{S}^{\prime}=(\epsilon^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
of
S
are
equivalent
if

(\pi_{S},\rho_{S})=(\pi_{S}^{\prime},\rho_{S}^{\prime})

as germs of maps defined near
S
. Equivalently,
T_{S}
is equivalent to
T_{S}^{\prime}
if there exist a smooth function
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}:S\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
satisfying
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\min(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
and a (nonlinear) bundle map

\tau:N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}S\to N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}S

which is fiberwise diffeomorphism such that
\|\tau(y)\|=\|y\|
, such that the linearization along the zero section

d\tau|_{S}:NS\to NS

is the identity map of
NS
, and such that

\varphi|_{N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}S}=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\tau|_{N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}S}.

If
T_{S}
and
T_{S}^{\prime}
are equivalent, we denote
T_{S}\sim T_{S}^{\prime}
.

Definition C.11
.

Let
M,P
be smooth manifolds and
f:M\to P
be a smooth map. Let
S\subseteq M
be a submanifold. We say a tubular neighborhood
T_{S}
is
compatible
with
f
if
f\circ\pi_{S}=f
in
|T_{S}|
.

Below is a lemma useful in the induction argument. As its argument will be used in the orbifold setting, we provide an alternate self-contained proof.

Lemma C.12
.

[
Mat12
, Proposition 6.2]

In the situation of Definition
C.11
, suppose restriction
f|_{S}:S\to P
is a submersion. Let
O\subseteq S
be an open subset and
D\subseteq S
is a closed subset contained in
O
. Suppose
T_{0}=(\epsilon_{0},\varphi_{0})
is a tubular neighborhood of
O
in
M
which is compatible with
f
, then there exists a tubular neighborhood
T_{S}=(\epsilon,\varphi)
of
S
which is compatible to
f
such that
T_{S}
and
T_{0}
are equivalent near
D
. In particular, the old and new tubular projections and tubular functions agree near
D
.

Proof.

For each
p\in P
, denote
M_{p}=f^{-1}(p)
and
S_{p}=f^{-1}(p)\cap S
. By the condition that
f|_{S}:S\to P
is a submersion,
S_{p}
is a smooth submanifold and
M_{p}
is a smooth submanifold near
S_{p}
. Moroever, the normal bundle of
S_{p}
inside
M_{p}
is isomorphic to
NS|_{S_{p}}
.

We first extend the tubular neighborhood in the linear level. Consider the exact sequence of vector bundles with a splitting
\tau:NS\to TM|_{S}

A splitting
\tau
is said to be compatible with
f
if for each
x\in S
,
\tau(N_{x}S)\subseteq T_{x}M_{f(x)}
. For the existing tubular neighborhood
T_{0}=(\epsilon_{0},\varphi_{0})
, the linearization

\tau_{0}:=d\varphi_{0}|_{O}:NS|_{O}\to TM|_{O}

gives a splitting of the exact sequence over
O
which is compatible with
f
. Then one can find a splitting
\tau_{1}:NS\to TM|_{S}
compatible with
f
such that
\tau_{1}
agrees with
\tau_{0}
over an open neighborhood
O^{\prime}\subseteq O
of
D
.

We use the extended splitting
\tau_{1}
to define a tubular neighborhood. For each
p\in P
, let
\exp_{M_{p}}
be the exponential map inside the submanifold
M_{p}
with respect to the restriction of
g^{TM}
to
M_{p}
. For
\epsilon:S\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
sufficiently small, define

\varphi_{1}:N^{\epsilon}S\to M,\ \varphi_{1}(x,v)=\exp_{M_{f(x)}}\big(\tau_{1}(x,v)\big)\in M_{f(x)}\subseteq M.

It is easy to check that this is a tubular neighborhood which is compatible with
f
. However it does not necessarily match with the existing one.

We interpolate
\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}
over the region
O^{\prime}\setminus D
. Choose a cut-off function
\nu_{O^{\prime},D}:S\to[0,1]
supported in
O^{\prime}
such that
\nu_{O^{\prime},D}\equiv 1
near
D
. For each
x\in O^{\prime}\setminus D
, compare the two fibers

\varphi_{1}(N_{x}^{\epsilon}S)\ {\rm and}\ \varphi_{0}(N_{x}^{\epsilon}S).

As
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{0}
has the same linearization along
O^{\prime}
, we see that for all
(x,v)\in NS|_{O^{\prime}}
, one has

d_{M_{f(x)}}\Big(\varphi_{0}(x,v),\varphi_{1}(x,v)\Big)=O(\|v\|^{2}).

Then if
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, for all
(x,v)\in N^{\epsilon}O^{\prime}
, there exists a unique shortest geodesic

\gamma_{(x,v)}:[0,1]\to M_{f(x)}

such that

\displaystyle\ \gamma_{(x,v)}(0)=\varphi_{0}(x,v),\

\displaystyle\ \gamma_{(x,v)}(1)=\varphi_{1}(x,v).

Then define
\varphi:N^{\epsilon}S\to M
by

\varphi(x,v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\varphi_{0}(x,v),&\ x\in D,\\
\gamma_{(x,v)}\big(1-\nu_{O^{\prime},D}(x)\big),&\ x\in O^{\prime}\setminus D,\\
\varphi_{1}(x,v),&\ x\notin O^{\prime}.\end{array}\right.

(See Figure
4
for an illustration of this interpolation.)

Figure 4.
The fibers of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{0}
over the same point are contained in the same preimage
M_{p}=f^{-1}(p)
. Their tangents at the zero section are the same so their distance is small compared to the distance from the zero section. One interpolates between them along the shortest geodesics
\gamma\subseteq M_{p}
in a region near
D
.

One can easily check that
\varphi
is a tubular neighborhood, compatible with
f
, and agrees with
\varphi_{0}
near
D
. Hence
T_{S}=(\epsilon,\varphi)
is a tubular neighborhood of
S
satisfying the desired conditions.
∎

C.2.2.
Normal bundles of the isotropy stratification

We would like to consider tubular neighborhoods of smooth manifolds inside an orbifold (without boundary). We first discuss the normal bundle to strata of the isotropy stratification (Lemma
2.12
). Here we do not consider the vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
and the isotropy stratification of
{\mathcal{U}}
means that for the rank zero vector bundle.

Let
\gamma
be an isotropy type represented by
(G_{0},V_{0})
and
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be the stratum of the isotropy stratification. In the normal direction, locally, a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
is a fiberwise quotient of a vector bundle by the group
G_{0}
. It is tempting to define a normal bundle to
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
as a fiberwise quotient of a global vector bundle over
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
such that for each orbifold chart
C=(G,U,\psi)
containing a point of
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
the vector bundle is the normal bundle over
U_{G}\subseteq U
. However, there is no canonical transition functions. Indeed, the correct viewpoint is that
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
is a non-effective orbifold and the local pieces
NU_{G}
define an orbifold vector bundle. However, we want to refrain from discussing non-effective orbifolds as we always do in this paper.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma}
be the set of charts of
{\mathcal{U}}
of the form
(G,U,\psi)
such that
G\cong G_{0}
and the fiber of
TU|_{U_{G}}
is isomorphic to
V_{0}
as representations. Then each such chart provides a manifold chart of
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
. Then define a space

{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}:=\left(\bigsqcup_{C=(G,U,\psi)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma}}NU_{G}\right)/\sim

where the equivalence relation is defined as follows: a point
(x_{1},v_{1})\in NU_{1,G_{1}}
(where
x_{1}\in U_{1,G_{1}}
and
v_{1}\in NU_{1,G_{1}}|_{x_{1}}
) coming from a chart
C_{1}=(G_{1},U_{1},\psi_{1})
and a point
(x_{2},v_{2})\in NU_{2,G_{2}}
coming from a chart
C_{2}=(G_{2},U_{2},\psi_{2})
are equivalent if there exists a chart
C=(G,U,\psi)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma}
,
(x,v)\in NU_{G}
, and chart embeddings

\iota_{i}:U\to U_{i},\ i=1,2

such that
\iota_{i}(x)=x_{i}
and
d\iota_{i}(x)(v)=v_{i}
. We claim that
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
is naturally an effective orbifold. Indeed, for each
C=(G,U,\psi)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma}
, the natural map

d\psi:NU_{G}\to{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}

is
G
-invariant and a homeomorphism onto an open subset. Hence
(G,NU_{G},d\psi)
is an orbifold chart of
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
. Moreover, the compatibility of charts in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma}
guarantees the compatibility of the collection of orbifold charts of
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
is an effective orbifold. Notice that there is a canonical projection map
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
and a canonical zero section
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}\to{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
. We call
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
the
normal bundle
to
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
. Notice that
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
has its isotropy stratification whose strata are indexed by isotropy types
\delta\geq\gamma
.

Moreover, suppose
{\mathcal{U}}
is equipped with a straightened Riemannian metric
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
, then there is a smooth function on
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}
whose pullback to each chart
(G,NU_{G},d\psi)
is the function
(x,v)\mapsto\|v\|^{2}
where
\|v\|
is calculated by the Riemannian metric.

C.2.3.
Tubular neighborhoods in orbifolds

Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be an effective orbifold equipped with a straightened Riemannian metric
g^{T{\mathcal{U}}}
. Within this appendix, a
submanifold
of
{\mathcal{U}}
means a submanifold of a stratum of the isotropy stratification of
{\mathcal{U}}
. On the other hand, let
P
be a smooth manifold. Recall that a continuous map
f:{\mathcal{U}}\to P
is called smooth if its pullback to each orbifold chart is a smooth map to
P
; it is a submersion if its pullback to each orbifold chart is a submersion (see Section
2
). As an example, the projection
{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
is a submersion.

Definition C.13
.

Let
Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a submanifold contained in
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
. Let
\pi_{N_{\gamma}S}:N_{\gamma}Z\to Z
be the normal bundle of
Z
inside
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma}
.
The
normal bundle
of a submanifold
Z
in
{\mathcal{U}}
is the orbifold

NZ:=\pi_{N_{\gamma}Z}^{*}{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}|_{Z}

with a natural projection map
\pi_{NZ}:NZ\to Z
. A point of
NZ
is denoted by
(x,v,w)
where
x\in Z
,
v\in N_{\gamma}Z|_{x}
, and
w\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\gamma}|_{x}
. Define

\rho_{NZ}(x,v,w):=\|v\|^{2}+\|w\|^{2}.

For each smooth function
\epsilon:Z\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, denote

N^{\epsilon}Z=\Big\{(x,v,w)\in NZ\ |\ \rho_{NZ}(x,v,w)<\epsilon(x)\Big\}.

Definition C.14
.

A
tubular neighborhood
of
Z
inside
{\mathcal{U}}
is a pair
T_{Z}=(\epsilon,\varphi)
where
\epsilon:Z\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a smooth function and

\varphi:N^{\epsilon}Z\to{\mathcal{U}}

is an orbifold open embedding satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

\varphi|_{Z}
is the inclusion of
Z
into
{\mathcal{U}}
.

(2)

For each
x\in Z
, if we lift
\varphi
to a chart
C=(G,U,\psi)
containing
x
, then
\varphi
is a
G
-equivariant tubular neighborhood of
\psi^{-1}(Z)
inside
U
.

(3)

Associated to each tubular neighborhood
T_{Z}
, define

|T_{Z}|:=\varphi(N^{\epsilon}Z)

over which there is a tubular projection
\pi_{Z}:|T_{Z}|\to Z
and a tubular function
\rho_{Z}:|T_{Z}|\to[0,+\infty)
. Both are smooth maps on orbifolds.

(4)

Two tubular neighborhoods
T_{Z}=(\epsilon,\varphi)
and
T_{Z}^{\prime}=(\epsilon^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
are equivalent if there exist a smooth function
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}:Z\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\leq\min(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
, an orbifold isomorphism

\tau:N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}Z\to N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}Z

satisfying 1)
\pi_{NZ}\circ\tau=\pi_{NZ}
, 2)
\rho_{NZ}\circ\tau=\rho_{NZ}
, 3), for each chart
C=(G,U,\psi)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\gamma}
,
\tau
is lifted to a
G
-invariant map
\tau:NU_{G}\to NU_{G}
such that the linearization along the zero section is the identity map of
NU_{G}
, and 4)

\varphi|_{N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}Z}=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\tau|_{N^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}Z}.

(5)

A tubular neighborhood
T_{Z}
is
compatible
with a smooth map
f:{\mathcal{U}}\to P
if
f\circ\pi_{Z}=f
on
|T_{Z}|
.

Now we can generalize the tubular neighborhood theorem to the orbifold setting.

Proposition C.15
.

Let
Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a submanifold. Let
f:{\mathcal{U}}\to P
be a smooth map such that
f|_{Z}:Z\to P
is a submersion. Let
O\subseteq Z
be an open subset and
D\subseteq Z
be a closed subset contained in
O
. Suppose
T_{0}=(\epsilon_{0},\varphi_{0})
is a tubular neighborhood of
O
which is compatible with
f
. Then there exists a tubular neighborhood
T_{Z}=(\rho,\varphi)
of
Z
compatible with
f
such that
T_{Z}
is equivalent to
T_{0}
near
D
.

Proof.

The argument is analogous to the manifold case. We construct the tubular neighborhood on the chart level and prove that the chartwise constructions are compatible with chart embeddings.

We introduce similar notations first. For each
x\in Z
, choose an orbifold chart
C=(G,U,\psi)
centered at
x
. Denote
\tilde{Z}=\psi^{-1}(Z)\subseteq U_{G}
,
\tilde{O}=\psi^{-1}(O)
and
\tilde{D}=\psi^{-1}(D)
. Then by definition
\tilde{f}:=f\circ\psi:U\to P
is smooth. For each
p
, denote
U_{p}=\tilde{f}^{-1}(p)
and
\tilde{Z}_{p}=U_{p}\cap\tilde{Z}
. Any smooth function
\epsilon:Z\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is lifted to a smooth function
\epsilon:\tilde{Z}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Then the existing tubular neighborhood
T_{0}
is lifted to

\tilde{\varphi}_{0}:N^{\epsilon}\tilde{O}\to U

after appropriately shrinking the function
\epsilon
.

Consider the
G
-equivariant exact sequence

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T\tilde{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.28821pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.28821pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{TU|_{\tilde{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 128.49442pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 128.49442pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{N\tilde{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 173.17496pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 173.17496pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

A
G
-equivariant splitting
\tau:N\tilde{Z}\to TU|_{\tilde{Z}}
is said to be compatible with
f
if for each
x\in\tilde{Z}
,
\tau(N_{x}\tilde{Z})\subseteq TU_{f(\psi(x))}
. Then the linearization of
\tilde{\varphi}_{0}
defines a splitting over
\tilde{O}
which is compatible with
f
, denoted by
\tau_{0}
. Then we can choose a
G
-equivariant splitting

\tau:N\tilde{Z}\to TU|_{\tilde{Z}}

which coincides with
\tau_{0}
over an open neighborhood
\tilde{O}^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{O}
of
\tilde{D}
. Then define the map

\tilde{\varphi}_{1}:N^{\epsilon}\tilde{Z}\to U,\ \tilde{\varphi}_{1}(x,v)=\exp_{U_{\tilde{f}(x)}}\big(\tau(x,v)\big)\in U_{\tilde{f}(x)}.

Next, similar to the proof of Lemma
C.12
, we interpolate
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{0}
within
\tilde{O}^{\prime}\setminus\tilde{D}
. Indeed, if
x\in\tilde{O}^{\prime}
, then

d_{U_{\tilde{f}(x)}}\left(\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(x,v),\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(x,v)\right)=O(\|v\|^{2}).

Hence there is a shortest geodesic
\gamma_{(x,v)}:[0,1]\to U_{\tilde{f}(x)}
connecting
\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(x,v)
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(x,v)
. Choose a smooth cut-off function
\nu_{\tilde{O}^{\prime},\tilde{D}}:\tilde{Z}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
supported inside
\tilde{O}^{\prime}
such that
\nu_{\tilde{O}^{\prime},\tilde{D}}\equiv 1
near
\tilde{D}
. Then define

U_{\tilde{f}(x)}\ni\tilde{\varphi}(x,v)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(x,v),&\ x\in\tilde{D},\\
\gamma_{(x,v)}\big(1-\nu_{\tilde{O}^{\prime},\tilde{D}}(x)\big),&\ x\in\tilde{O}\setminus\tilde{D},\\
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}(x,v),&\ x\notin\tilde{O}.\end{array}\right.

As the submanifolds
U_{\tilde{f}(x)}
are
G
-invariant and the Riemannian metric is
G
-invariant, the chartwise tubular neighborhoods are
G
-equivariant. Also easy to check that these tubular neighborhoods are compatible with chart embedding. Hence this is a tubular neighborhood of
Z
. From the construction we can see it agrees with
T_{0}
near
D
.
∎

C.3.
Whitney stratified sets in orbifolds are Thom–Mather sets

Definition C.16
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be an effective orbifold (without boundary). A
Whitney stratified subset
of
{\mathcal{U}}
is a subset
Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
equipped with a stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

Each stratum
Z_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a submanifold contained in a stratum
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma(\alpha)}
of the isotropy stratification.

(2)

For each orbifold chart
C=(G,U,\psi)
, the above conditions imply that for each stratum
Z_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}
,
\psi^{-1}(Z_{\alpha})\subseteq U
is a (possibly empty) smooth submanifold. Then for each pair
Z_{\alpha}<Z_{\beta}
,
(\psi^{-1}(Z_{\beta}),\psi^{-1}(Z_{\alpha}))
is Whitney regular.

Definition C.17
.

Let
(Z,{\mathfrak{Z}})
be a Whitney stratified subset of
{\mathcal{U}}
. A set of
ambient control data
for
{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a collection
{\mathfrak{I}}=\{T_{\alpha}\ |\ Z_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{S}}\}
of tubular neighborhoods
T_{\alpha}
of
Z_{\alpha}
such that whenever
Z_{\alpha}<Z_{\beta}
, one has

\displaystyle\ \pi_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\beta}=\pi_{\alpha},\

\displaystyle\ \rho_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\beta}=\rho_{\alpha}

as germs of maps defined near
Z_{\alpha}
.

The following is an obvious consequence of Definition
C.1
and Definition
C.17
.

Proposition C.18
.

Suppose one has a set of ambient control data
{\mathfrak{I}}=\{(T_{\alpha})\ |\ Z_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}\}
for
(Z,{\mathfrak{Z}})
. Let
{\mathfrak{J}}
be the data

{\mathfrak{J}}=\Big\{(\pi_{\alpha}|_{Z},\rho_{\alpha}|_{Z})\ |\ Z_{\alpha}\in{\mathfrak{Z}}\Big\}.

Then the triple
(Z,{\mathfrak{Z}},{\mathfrak{J}})
is a Thom–Mather stratified space.

Our theorem is

Theorem C.19
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be an orbifold (without boundary) and
(Z,{\mathfrak{Z}})
is a closed Whitney stratified subset. Then there exists a set of ambient control data for
{\mathfrak{Z}}
.

The proof resembles that of
[
Mat12
, Proposition 7.1]
. Since the proof (as well as Mather’s proof) is complicated, we would like to demonstrate the main idea for the simple case when there are only three strata
Z_{1}<Z_{2}<Z_{3}
. We can first construct a tubular neighborhood
T_{1}=(\epsilon_{1},\varphi_{1})
of
Z_{1}
, which comes with the tubular projection

\pi_{1}:|T_{1}|\to Z_{1}

and tubular function

\rho_{1}:|T_{1}|\to[0,+\infty).

Lemma C.20
.

There exists an open neighborhood
O_{1}\subseteq|T_{1}|
of
Z_{1}
such that

(\pi_{1},\rho_{1}):Z_{2}\cap O_{1}\to Z_{1}\times(0,+\infty)

is a submersion.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the assertion locally. For each
p\in Z_{1}
, choose an orbifold chart
C=(G,U,\psi)
containing
p
so that
\psi^{-1}(Z_{1})\subseteq U_{G}
is a submanifold. The two maps
\pi_{1}
and
\rho_{1}
are lifted to
G
-invariant maps near
\psi^{-1}(Z_{1})
. Denote them by
\tilde{\pi}_{1}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{1}
. On the other hand,
\psi^{-1}(Z_{2})\subseteq U
is a
G
-invariant smooth submanifold. By definition,
(\psi^{-1}(Z_{2}),\psi^{-1}(Z_{1}))
satisfies Whitney’s condition (b) at each point of
\psi^{-1}(Z_{1})
. This implies (see
[
Mat73
, Lemma 7.3]
) that there exists a
G
-invariant neighborhood
\tilde{O}_{1}
of
\psi^{-1}(Z_{1})
such that the projection

(\tilde{\pi}_{1},\tilde{\rho}_{1}):\psi^{-1}(Z_{2})\cap\tilde{O}_{1}\to\psi^{-1}(Z_{1})\times(0,+\infty)

is a submersion. This is equivalent to say that
(\pi_{1},\rho_{1})
is a submersion in
Z_{2}\cap\psi(\tilde{O}_{1})
. Then
O_{1}
can be constructed by taking union of these local pieces.
∎

Then by the relative case of Proposition
C.15
, if we shrink
|T_{1}|
appropriately (by taking a function
\epsilon_{1}^{\prime}\leq\epsilon_{1}
), then there exists a tubular neighborhood
T_{2}
of
Z_{2}\cap|T_{1}|
which is compatible with
(\pi_{1},\rho_{1})
. Then use the absolute case of Proposition
C.15
again, we can extend it to a tubular neighborhood
T_{2}
of
Z_{2}
which is compatible with
T_{1}
near
Z_{1}
.

Now we consider the third stratum
Z_{3}
. Notice that we can freely shrink
|T_{1}|
and
|T_{2}|
without altering the compatibility between
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
. First consider the open subset
Z_{3}\cap|T_{2}|\subseteq Z_{3}
. In the same way as before, after shrinking
|T_{2}|
appropriately, there exists a tubular neighborhood
T_{3}
of
Z_{3}\cap|T_{2}|
inside
|T_{2}|
which is compatible with
T_{2}
. We need to show that this is also compatible with
T_{1}
. Indeed,

\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{3}=(\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{2})\circ\pi_{3}=\pi_{1}\circ(\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{3})=\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{2}=\pi_{1},

where the equality holds near
|T_{1}|\cap Z_{2}
. Similarly
\rho_{1}\circ\pi_{3}=\rho_{1}
over the same region. Therefore, the tubular neighborhood
T_{3}
is compatible with
(\pi_{1},\rho_{1})
.

Then by Proposition
C.15
, after appropriately shrinking
|T_{1}|
and
|T_{2}|
, one can find a tubular neighborhood
T_{3}
of
Z_{3}
within
|T_{1}|\cup|T_{2}|
. Using this proposition again, we can construct a global tubular neighborhood
T_{3}
which is compatible with both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
. This finishes the proof in this sample case.

Proof of Theorem
C.19
.

We follow the proof of Mather
[
Mat12
, Section 7]
. Suppose
Z
has dimension
n
and let
Z_{k}\subseteq Z
be the union of strata of dimension at most
k
and
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k}
be the restricted stratification. We construct inductively for
k
. The induction hypothesis is that there exists a set of ambient control data
{\mathfrak{I}}_{k}
for
(Z_{k},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k})
(which is itself a Whitney stratified set in
{\mathcal{U}}
). Now we construct a set of ambient control data
{\mathfrak{I}}_{k+1}
for
(Z_{k+1},{\mathfrak{Z}}_{k+1})
which extends
{\mathfrak{I}}_{k}
. Notice that it is always the freedom to shrink the existing tubular neighborhood without breaking the compatibility conditions.

We construct
{\mathfrak{I}}_{k+1}
. As strata of the same dimension do not intersect, one can construct a tubular neighborhood for each individual strata of dimension
k+1
separately. Without loss of generality, assume that there is only one stratum
Z_{\beta}
of dimension
k+1
. The construction of the tubular neighborhood
T_{\beta}
uses a reversed induction on
l=0,1,\ldots,k+1
. For each
l
, denote

O_{l}=\bigcup_{l\leq{\rm dim}(Z_{\alpha})\leq k}|T_{\alpha}|

which is an open subset of
{\mathcal{U}}
. We construct inductively a tubular neighborhood
T_{\beta,l}
of
Z_{\beta}\cap O_{l}
inside
O_{l}
which is compatible with all existing tubular neighborhoods. The
l=k+1
case has nothing to prove. Suppose we have constructed a tubular neighborhood for
Z_{\beta}\cap T_{l+1}
satisfying the requirement. Then for each
Z_{\alpha}
of dimension
l
, we can check that

\pi_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\beta}=\pi_{\alpha},\ \rho_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\beta}=\rho_{\alpha}

near
Z_{\alpha}
. Therefore,
T_{\beta,l+1}
is compatible with
(\pi_{\alpha},\rho_{\alpha})
. Then one can extend
T_{\beta,l+1}
to a tubular neighborhood
T_{\beta,l}
of
Z_{\beta}\cap O_{l}
inside
O_{l}
using Proposition
C.15
. Then finally the tubular neighborhood
T_{\beta,0}
is a tubular neighborhood of
Z_{\beta}
inside
{\mathcal{U}}
which is compatible with all existing tubular neighborhoods. The induction on
k
can be carried on and stops after finitely many stages.
∎

C.3.1.
The case for orbifolds with boundary

We extend Definition
C.16
and Theorem
C.19
to the case when the orbifold has boundary. Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be an orbifold with boundary and
Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a subset. Denote

\displaystyle\ {\rm Int}Z:=Z\cap{\rm Int}{\mathcal{U}},\

\displaystyle\ \partial Z:=Z\cap\partial{\mathcal{U}}.

If
{\mathfrak{Z}}
is a stratification on
Z
with strata
Z_{\alpha}
, then denote

\displaystyle\ {\rm Int}Z_{\alpha}:=Z_{\alpha}\cap{\rm Int}{\mathcal{U}},\ \partial Z_{\alpha}:=Z_{\alpha}\cap\partial{\mathcal{U}}.

Definition C.21
.

A
collared Whitney stratified subset
of
{\mathcal{U}}
is a subset
Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
equipped with a stratification
{\mathfrak{Z}}
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

The interior
{\rm Int}Z
with the induced stratification is a Whitney stratified subset of
{\rm Int}{\mathcal{U}}
. Let
{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma(\alpha)}
be the stratum of
{\mathcal{U}}
whose interior contains
{\rm Int}Z_{\alpha}
.

(2)

\partial Z_{\alpha}
is a submanifold of
\partial{\mathcal{U}}
contained in
\partial{\mathcal{U}}_{\gamma(\alpha)}
.

(3)

There is an open embedding
\iota:\partial{\mathcal{U}}\times[0,\epsilon)\to{\mathcal{U}}
(a collar neighborhood of the boundary) sending
\partial{\mathcal{U}}\times\{0\}
identically onto
\partial{\mathcal{U}}
such that the restriction of
\iota
onto
\partial Z_{\alpha}\times[0,\epsilon)
is an open embedding into
Z_{\alpha}
.

If
Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
is a collared Whitney stratified subset, then one can refine the stratification as

Z=\bigsqcup_{\alpha}{\rm Int}Z_{\alpha}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\alpha}\partial Z_{\alpha}.

Corollary C.22
.

Let
Z\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
be a collared Whitney stratified subset. Then
Z
with the refined stratification specified above admits a set of ambient control data.

Proof.

The proof can be carried out in the same way as that of Theorem
C.19
. The collared structure allows one to choose product type ambient control data near boundary strata.
∎
